Numbers — Saints Bible cross-references
Completed: Numbers 1:1–36:13. Book initial curated pass complete. Overall next: 1 Kings 1:1.
1287 completed source verses; 1853 completed cross-reference pairs.
Completed means the initial curated cross-reference pass: the source verse and every listed target passage were read in the pinned Saints-Translation English records, and each retained pair has a specific explanation. It does not mean every possible biblical connection has been exhausted, or independent scholarly approval.
6 target candidate(s) await source evidence or identity review and are excluded from completed pair totals; see source-checks.json.
Reference edition: Saints Holy Bible 70-book edition, pinned Saints-Translation English records. Greek-based source numbering is retained.

Numbers 1:1
And the Lord spoke unto Moses in the wilderness of Sinai, in the Tent of Testimony, on the first day of the second month of the second year after they came out of the land of Egypt, saying:
  Exodus 40:17 — Earlier tent chronology: The tent was erected in the first month of the second year; this census revelation occurs there in that year’s second month.
  Numbers 10:11-12 — Later Sinai departure: The census begins on the second month’s first day at Sinai; the later departure begins there on that month’s twentieth day.

Numbers 1:2
Take the sum of the whole congregation of the sons of Israel, by their families, by their ancestral houses, according to the number of their names, head by head, every male
  Numbers 26:2 — Later census mandate: Both mandates count Israel by ancestral houses; the later command explicitly repeats the twenty-year military criterion continued in the next source verse.

Numbers 1:3
from twenty years old and upward, every one who goes out in Israel’s army: register them with their army; you and Aaron shall register them.
  Numbers 26:2 — Repeated military census criterion: Both census commands count Israelites from twenty years upward who go out for battle, although the later census involves Eleazar rather than Aaron.
  Exodus 30:14 — Earlier census age criterion: The earlier census contribution applies from twenty years upward, the same lower age boundary used here for military registration.

Numbers 1:4
And one of the rulers from each tribe shall be with you; they shall be according to their ancestral houses.
  Numbers 7:2 — Census rulers identified later: The dedication account identifies its twelve ancestral-house rulers as the tribal rulers who stood over the enrolment, the office prescribed here.

Numbers 1:5
And these are the names of the men who shall stand with you. For Reuben: Elizur son of Shedeur.
  Numbers 2:10 — Census leader and camp ruler: Elizur son of Shedeur, selected here for Reuben’s census, is named again as that same tribe’s ruler in its camp arrangement.
  Numbers 7:30 — Census leader and dedication donor: The dedication list names Elizur son of Shedeur as Reuben’s ruler, identifying the same named tribal representative appointed here.
  Numbers 10:18 — Census leader and march commander: Reuben’s army later marches under Elizur son of Shedeur, the same man named here to assist with that tribe’s registration.

Numbers 1:6
For Simeon: Shelumiel son of Zurishaddai.
  Numbers 2:12 — Census leader and camp ruler: Shelumiel son of Zurishaddai, selected here for Simeon’s census, is named again as that same tribe’s ruler in its camp arrangement.
  Numbers 7:36 — Census leader and dedication donor: The dedication list names Shelumiel son of Zurishaddai as Simeon’s ruler, identifying the same named tribal representative appointed here.
  Numbers 10:19 — Census leader and march commander: Simeon’s army later marches under Shelumiel son of Zurishaddai, the same man named here to assist with that tribe’s registration.

  Numbers 10:14 — Census leader and march commander: Judah’s army later marches under Nahshon son of Amminadab, the same man named here to assist with that tribe’s registration.

Numbers 1:16
These are the men called from the congregation, rulers of the tribes by ancestral houses; they are commanders of Israel’s thousands.
  Numbers 7:2 — Rulers and their census office: The dedication narrative explicitly identifies its twelve ancestral-house rulers as those who stood over the enrolment, matching these summoned tribal commanders.

Numbers 1:17
And Moses and Aaron took these men who had been called by name.
  Numbers 1:5-16 — Same-chapter named appointments carried out: Moses and Aaron take the men called by name; the preceding list supplies those individual names and identifies their tribal offices.

Numbers 1:18
And they assembled the whole congregation on the first day of the month of the second year and registered them by their descent and ancestral houses, counting their names, from twenty years old and upward, every male, head by head.
  Numbers 1:2-3 — Same-chapter census command fulfilled: The gathering and registration by descent, ancestral houses, individual names, and age twenty upward implements the census procedure commanded earlier in this chapter.

Numbers 1:19
As the Lord instructed Moses, they were registered in the wilderness of Sinai.
  Numbers 1:2-3 — Same-chapter census instruction fulfilled: This statement of registration at the Lord’s instruction concludes the execution of the specifically prescribed ancestral-house and military-age census.

Numbers 1:20
And the sons of Reuben, Israel’s firstborn, were reckoned by their families, by their divisions, by their ancestral houses, by the number of their names, head by head, every male from twenty years old and upward, every one who goes out in the army:
  Numbers 26:5-7 — Later census of the same tribe: The later census records Reuben’s named clans and their registration, continuing the tribal and ancestral-family organization specified for this earlier military census.
  Exodus 6:14 — Reuben’s firstborn and family identity: Both passages identify Reuben as Israel’s firstborn and organize his descendants by ancestral family, with the earlier genealogy naming his four sons.

Numbers 1:21
The enrolment from the tribe of Reuben was forty-six thousand five hundred.
  Numbers 2:11 — Identical census figure in camp arrangement: Reuben’s military enrolment is forty-six thousand five hundred here; the camp arrangement assigns precisely that same registered number to Reuben’s army.
  Numbers 26:7 — Contrasting later census figure: The earlier Reuben registration is forty-six thousand five hundred; the later census gives forty-three thousand seven hundred thirty for the same tribe, recording a different enrolment.

Numbers 1:44
This was the enrolment made by Moses, Aaron, and the rulers of Israel, twelve men, one man for each tribe, according to the tribe of their ancestral houses.
  Numbers 7:2 — Twelve rulers and census responsibility: This summary counts twelve rulers assisting the enrolment; the dedication account calls its twelve ancestral-house rulers those who stood over that enrolment.

Numbers 1:45
And the whole enrolment of the sons of Israel with their army, from twenty years old and upward, every one who goes out to battle in Israel,
  Numbers 26:2 — Repeated military eligibility: The summary includes everyone twenty years upward going out to battle, the same eligibility explicitly required for the later census.

Numbers 1:46
was six hundred three thousand five hundred fifty.
  Numbers 2:32 — Identical aggregate in camp census: The military census total of six hundred three thousand five hundred fifty is repeated exactly as the combined enrolment of the camps.
  Exodus 39:3 — Earlier identical census total: The tent silver account records six hundred three thousand five hundred fifty enrolled men from twenty upward, the same aggregate reported here.
  Numbers 26:51 — Contrasting later census total: The Sinai census totals six hundred three thousand five hundred fifty, whereas the later registration totals six hundred one thousand seven hundred thirty.

Numbers 1:47
But the Levites, from their ancestral tribe, were not registered among the sons of Israel.
  Numbers 2:33 — Levites excluded from military camp total: Both statements explicitly exclude the Levites from registration together with the other Israelites, separating their count from the military camp enrolment.
  Numbers 26:62 — Later separate Levitical registration: The later census likewise excludes Levites from the Israelite registration, while separately counting males from one month and explaining their lack of land allotment.

Numbers 1:48
And the Lord spoke unto Moses, saying:
  Numbers 1:49-53 — Same-chapter speech introduction and particular charge: This introduction announces the Lord’s specific instruction to exclude Levi from the military census and assign the tribe responsibility around the Tent of Testimony.

Numbers 1:49
See that you do not register the tribe of Levi together with them, neither shall you take their number among the sons of Israel.
  Numbers 26:62 — Repeated exclusion from general census: The later registration again counts Levites separately from Israel, specifying their own one-month age class and their exclusion from an allotment.

Numbers 1:50
And you shall appoint the Levites over the Tent of Testimony, and over all its furnishings, and over all that is therein. They shall carry the Tent and all its furnishings, and they shall minister therein, and shall camp round about the Tent.
  Numbers 3:7-8 — Expanded Levitical custody charge: The general appointment over the tent and its furnishings becomes the charge to perform its work and guard its furnishings before Aaron.
  Numbers 4:49 — Individual tent service assignments: The later service census assigns each Levite his particular work and burden, implementing this general appointment to minister and transport the tent.

Numbers 1:51
And when the Tent sets out, the Levites shall take it down; when it camps, they shall erect it. Let an outsider who approaches die.
  Numbers 10:17-21 — Tent dismantling and transport in departure: The departure account assigns tent transport to Gershon and Merari and holy-object transport to Kohath, describing the movement entrusted generally to Levites here.
  Numbers 4:15 — Holy objects and lethal approach boundary: Kohath may carry the covered holy things but must not touch them lest they die, specifying a transport boundary alongside this outsider prohibition.

Numbers 1:52
And the sons of Israel shall camp, each in his own formation and each according to his own command, with their army.
  Numbers 2:2 — Camp formation instruction expanded: The next camp instruction explicitly associates each formation with ancestral-house signs and locates the Israelite camps around the Tent of Testimony.

Numbers 1:53
But let the Levites camp before the Lord round about the Tent of Testimony, and there shall be no offence among the sons of Israel. And the Levites themselves shall keep the charge of the Tent of Testimony.
  Numbers 18:3-5 — Guarding the sanctuary prevents wrath: The later charge assigns Levites tent custody, restricts access to holy vessels and altar, and connects guarding holy things with preventing wrath in Israel.
  Numbers 3:38 — Sanctuary guard and lethal outsider boundary: Moses, Aaron, and his sons keep the sanctuary’s charge on the tent’s east, while outsiders face death; this supplies a particular part of the protective arrangement.

Numbers 1:54
And the sons of Israel did everything that the Lord commanded Moses and Aaron; so they did.
  Numbers 1:18-19 — Same-chapter obedience summary and census execution: The chapter’s obedience summary includes the gathering and registration that the preceding account explicitly describes as performed according to the Lord’s instructions.

Numbers 2:1
And the Lord spoke unto Moses and Aaron, saying:
  Numbers 2:2-9 — Same-chapter speech introduction and first camp formation: This speech introduction leads into the ancestral-house camp arrangement and its first specified formation, Judah’s eastern camp with Issachar and Zebulun.

Numbers 2:2
Let the sons of Israel camp, each beside his neighbour, by formation and by the signs of their ancestral houses. The sons of Israel shall camp opposite, around the Tent of Testimony.
  Numbers 1:52-53 — Camp organization and central Levitical guard: The earlier order separates Israel’s formations from the Levites guarding the tent; this instruction locates the ancestral-house formations around that same tent.

Numbers 2:3
And those camping first toward the east shall be the formation of Judah’s camp, with their army. The ruler of the sons of Judah is Nahshon son of Amminadab.
  Numbers 1:7 — Camp ruler and census representative: The camp ruler Nahshon son of Amminadab is the man earlier designated for Judah in the list assisting Moses and Aaron’s census.
  Numbers 10:14 — Camp command realized in departure: The later departure places Judah’s army under Nahshon son of Amminadab, carrying this named camp leadership into the recorded march.

Numbers 2:4
His army, those registered, numbered seventy-four thousand six hundred.
  Numbers 1:25 — Camp strength repeats tribal census: The camp army’s seventy-four thousand six hundred is the identical number recorded for Judah in the preceding chapter’s military registration.

Numbers 2:5
And those camping next shall be the tribe of Issachar; the ruler of the sons of Issachar is Nethanel son of Zuar.
  Numbers 1:8 — Camp ruler and census representative: The camp ruler Nethanel son of Zuar is the man earlier designated for Issachar in the list assisting Moses and Aaron’s census.
  Numbers 10:15 — Camp command realized in departure: The later departure places Issachar’s army under Nethanel son of Zuar, carrying this named camp leadership into the recorded march.

Numbers 2:6
His army, those registered, numbered fifty-four thousand four hundred.
  Numbers 1:27 — Camp strength repeats tribal census: The camp army’s fifty-four thousand four hundred is the identical number recorded for Issachar in the preceding chapter’s military registration.

Numbers 2:7

Numbers 2:8

Numbers 2:9
All those registered in the camp of Judah, with their army, numbered one hundred eighty-six thousand four hundred. They shall set out first.
  Numbers 10:14-16 — First camp’s departure order carried out: Judah’s camp is commanded to depart first with Issachar and Zebulun; the departure narrative names these same three armies in that first formation.
  Numbers 2:4-8 — Same-chapter camp subtotal and constituent counts: Judah’s one hundred eighty-six thousand four hundred combines Judah’s seventy-four thousand six hundred, Issachar’s fifty-four thousand four hundred, and Zebulun’s fifty-seven thousand four hundred.

Numbers 2:10

  Numbers 18:2 — Repeated Levites’ ministry before Aaron: The later instruction again tells Aaron to bring the tribe of Levi near, joining his ancestral brethren to him to minister before the Tent of Testimony.

Numbers 5:1
And the Lord spoke unto Moses, saying:
  Numbers 5:2-3 — Same-chapter introduction-to-specific-instruction: This speech introduces the removal of specified unclean persons and the protection of the Lord's dwelling among Israel.

Numbers 5:2
Command the sons of Israel to send out of the camp everyone with leprosy, everyone with a seminal discharge, and everyone unclean through a dead person.
  Leviticus 13:45-46 — parallel-camp-exclusion: Leviticus specifically requires the person with leprosy to dwell outside the camp, matching one class excluded here.
  Leviticus 15:2-4 — identified-uncleanness: The flow and seminal-discharge uncleanness defined in Leviticus supplies another category expressly sent outside the camp here.
  Numbers 19:11-13 — corpse-uncleanness: Numbers later defines corpse-contact uncleanness and warns that failure to purify defiles the Tent of the Lord.

Numbers 5:3
From male unto female, send them outside the camp, and they shall in no wise defile their camps, wherein I dwell among them.
  Deuteronomy 23:11-15 — divine-presence-and-camp-holiness: Both passages require exclusion associated with uncleanness because the Lord is present within Israel's camp.
  Numbers 19:20 — defilement-of-divine-dwelling: The camp's protection from defilement corresponds to the later warning against unpurified persons defiling the Lord's holy things.

Numbers 5:4
And the sons of Israel did so and sent them outside the camp. As the Lord spoke unto Moses, so the sons of Israel did.
  Numbers 5:2-3 — Same-chapter command-fulfilment: Israel's removal of the unclean persons carries out the immediately preceding instruction concerning the Lord's camp.
  Numbers 12:14-15 — later-particular-observance: Miriam's exclusion outside the camp and eventual return illustrate a later particular application of camp separation.

Numbers 5:5
And the Lord spoke unto Moses, saying:
  Numbers 5:6-8 — Same-chapter introduction-to-specific-instruction: This speech introduces guilt, confession, restitution with an added fifth, and the case lacking a recipient's next of kin.

Numbers 5:6
Speak unto the sons of Israel, saying: If a man or woman commits any of the sins of men, and acts with utter disregard, and that soul incurs guilt,
  Leviticus 5:21-24 — parallel-guilt-and-restitution: Leviticus specifies fraudulent wrongs against a neighbour and requires repayment with an added fifth when guilt is incurred.
  Numbers 5:7 — Same-chapter case-and-required-remedy: The guilt incurred by a man or woman leads to confession and restitution to the wronged person.

Numbers 5:7
that person shall confess the sin committed, and repay the principal of the offence, and add a fifth part thereto, and give it unto the one who was wronged.
  Leviticus 5:24 — parallel-restitution-rate: Both rules require repayment of the principal plus a fifth to the person to whom it belongs.
  Leviticus 5:5 — confession-requirement: Both instructions require the guilty person to confess the particular sin that has been committed.

Numbers 5:8
But if the man has no next of kin to receive repayment for the offence, the repayment made unto the Lord shall be the priest’s, besides the ram of atonement, wherewith he shall make atonement for him.
  Leviticus 5:25-26 — atoning-ram: The ram accompanying restitution corresponds to the guilt-offering ram through which the priest makes atonement in Leviticus.
  Leviticus 5:24 — modified-restitution-recipient: Leviticus requires payment to the owner; Numbers specifies the priestly recipient when no next of kin can receive repayment.

Numbers 5:9

  Numbers 18:8-9 — priestly-consecrated-share: The priest's entitlement to consecrated first portions is expanded by the later grant of Israel's holy offerings to Aaron.
  Leviticus 7:14 — particular-priestly-portion: The portion set apart from deliverance gifts supplies a specific example of an offering belonging to the priest.

Numbers 5:10

  Numbers 18:14 — consecrated-property-entitlement: The later grant of everything devoted to Aaron parallels the priest's entitlement to what a person gives him here.
  Leviticus 7:9 — particular-priestly-gift: Leviticus assigns specified prepared sacrifices to the officiating priest, a particular case of gifts belonging to him.

Numbers 5:11

  Numbers 5:12-15 — Same-chapter introduction-to-specific-case: This speech introduces marital transgression or suspicion and the husband's presentation of his wife and jealousy offering.

Numbers 5:12

  Numbers 5:29 — Same-chapter case-and-summary: The law's concluding summary returns to the opening case of a wife transgressing while under her husband.
  Leviticus 20:10 — contrasting-adultery-procedures: Leviticus prescribes punishment for adultery, while this law proceeds through the following concealed-act and jealousy conditions.

Numbers 5:13
and anyone lies with her with an emission of semen, and it escapes the eyes of her husband, and she conceals it, though she is defiled, and there is no witness concerning her, and she is not caught,
  Deuteronomy 19:15 — contrasting-evidentiary-conditions: This case expressly lacks a witness or discovery, whereas Deuteronomy describes establishing a matter through multiple witnesses.

Numbers 5:14

  Numbers 5:28 — Same-chapter jealousy-does-not-establish-guilt: The jealous husband's wife may be undefiled, and the later instruction expressly provides an unharmed outcome for that condition.
  Numbers 5:30 — Same-chapter case-and-summary: The concluding summary repeats the husband's spirit of jealousy as the circumstance for bringing his wife before the Lord.

Numbers 5:15

  Leviticus 2:1 — contrasting-offering-composition: The jealousy gift omits the oil and frankincense that accompany the ordinary fine-flour gift described in Leviticus.

  Numbers 5:27 — Same-chapter conditional-guilt-and-outcome: The oath's condition of defilement by someone besides her husband corresponds to the later conditional adverse result.

Numbers 5:21

  Numbers 5:27 — Same-chapter curse-and-conditional-fulfilment: The threatened collapsed thigh, swollen belly, and curse among the people recur in the result if concealed defilement occurred.

  Numbers 5:27 — Same-chapter curse-and-conditional-fulfilment: The water's threatened swelling and thigh collapse recur in the outcome expressly conditioned on concealed defilement.

And the priest shall take the sacrifice of jealousy from the woman’s hand, place the sacrifice before the Lord, and bring it near the altar.
  Numbers 5:18 — Same-chapter staged-offering-procedure: The priest now takes the jealousy offering that the preceding instruction placed in the woman's hands.
  Numbers 5:26 — Same-chapter altar-presentation-and-memorial: Bringing the jealousy gift near the altar precedes taking and offering its handful as a memorial portion.

Numbers 5:26
And the priest shall take a handful from the sacrifice as its memorial portion and offer it up on the altar. Afterward he shall make the woman drink the water.
  Leviticus 5:12 — parallel-memorial-handful: Both procedures require the priest to take a handful from the flour gift and offer its memorial portion on the altar.
  Leviticus 2:2 — parallel-memorial-handful: The priestly handful and altar memorial parallel the ordinary flour-offering procedure, whose oil and frankincense differ from jealousy's gift.

Numbers 5:27
And it shall be, if she is defiled and has concealed it from her husband, that the curse-bringing water of testing shall enter her, and her belly shall swell, and her thigh shall collapse, and the woman shall become a curse among her people.
  Numbers 5:13 — Same-chapter conditional-result-and-opening-case: The adverse result is expressly tied to the concealed defilement specified when the law first describes the case.
  Numbers 5:21-22 — Same-chapter conditional-fulfilment-of-curse: The conditional adverse outcome repeats the bodily effects and public curse pronounced in the priest's oath.

Numbers 5:28
But if the woman is not defiled, and is clean, she shall be unharmed, and shall bring forth seed.
  Numbers 5:19 — Same-chapter outcome-and-oath-protection: The clean woman's unharmed outcome fulfils the oath's stated protection when no sexual defilement has occurred.
  Numbers 5:14 — Same-chapter innocent-branch-of-jealousy-case: This unharmed result answers the opening possibility that a husband's jealousy concerns a wife who is not defiled.

Numbers 5:29
This is the law of jealousy, when a wife transgresses while under a husband and becomes defiled,
  Numbers 5:12-13 — Same-chapter summary-to-specific-case: The summary of jealousy law recalls the opening description of a wife's concealed marital transgression and defilement.
  Numbers 5:27 — Same-chapter summary-to-conditional-result: The summary's condition of a defiled wife corresponds to the stated conditional adverse result, rather than every instance of jealousy.

Numbers 5:30
or when a spirit of jealousy comes upon a man and he is jealous of his wife. He shall set his wife before the Lord, and the priest shall perform all this law for her.
  Numbers 5:14-16 — Same-chapter summary-to-specific-procedure: The summary repeats the jealousy circumstance and the presentation before the Lord specified at the beginning of the procedure.
  Numbers 5:25-26 — Same-chapter summary-to-required-priestly-action: The priest's performance of the law includes the specified altar presentation, memorial handful, and subsequent administration of water.

Numbers 5:31
And the man shall be free from sin, and that woman shall bear her sin.
  Numbers 5:27-28 — Same-chapter liability-and-conditional-outcomes: The closing liability statement follows distinct guilty and clean outcomes, so the woman's bearing sin is read with those conditions.

Numbers 6:1
And the Lord spoke unto Moses, saying:
  Numbers 6:2-4 — Same-chapter speech introduction: This divine speech opens the particular instruction that permits either sex to undertake consecration and forbids products of the vine.

Numbers 6:2
Speak unto the sons of Israel, and you shall say unto them: If a man or woman solemnly vows a vow of consecration unto the Lord, to be set apart
  Judges 13:5-7 — Consecration parallel: The voluntary consecration vow here illuminates Samson's designation as a Nazirite of God, while his lifelong calling differs from a limited vow.
  Acts 21:23-26 — Vow observance: The consecration vow of a man or woman supplies the legal setting for the four vowed men whose purification and offerings Paul supports.

Numbers 6:3
from wine and strong drink, he shall be purified from wine. He shall drink neither vinegar of wine, nor vinegar of strong drink, nor anything prepared from grapes; and he shall eat neither fresh grapes nor raisins.
  Judges 13:14 — Specific abstinence parallel: The consecrated person's ban on wine, strong drink, and vine products closely matches the messenger's restrictions upon Samson's mother.
  Luke 1:15 — Specific abstinence parallel: The vow excludes wine and strong drink; the announcement about John likewise expressly forbids both, without explicitly calling him a Nazirite.
  Amos 2:11-12 — Violation of consecration: The vow forbids wine, whereas Amos accuses Israel of giving its consecrated young men wine to drink.

Numbers 6:4
All the days of his vow he shall consume nothing made from the vine: wine, from pressed grape skins unto the seed.
  Judges 13:14 — Specific abstinence parallel: The exclusion of everything made from the vine is repeated in the messenger's command that Samson's mother eat nothing from the vine.

Numbers 6:5
All the days of the vow of purification no razor shall come upon his head. Until the days which he vowed unto the Lord are fulfilled, he shall be holy, letting the hair of his head grow long.
  Judges 16:17 — Explicit vow parallel: The consecrated person's uncut hair and prohibition of a razor are the very marks Samson cites when explaining his Nazirite identity.
  Judges 13:5 — Explicit vow parallel: The law prohibits a razor throughout the vow; the messenger similarly says no iron shall come upon the consecrated child's head.

Numbers 6:6
All the days of his vow unto the Lord he shall not enter where any dead person is.
  Leviticus 21:11 — Purity parallel: The vowed person must not enter where a dead person is, the same specific restriction imposed upon the high priest.
  Numbers 19:14 — Purity background: The ban on entering a place with a dead person corresponds to the rule that everyone entering a house containing a corpse becomes unclean.

Numbers 6:7
For his father, or his mother, or his brother, or his sister, he shall not defile himself when they die; for the vow of his God is upon him, upon his head.
  Leviticus 21:11-12 — Consecration parallel: Both the vowed person and the high priest must avoid corpse defilement even for parents because consecration rests upon them.
  Leviticus 21:1-3 — Purity contrast: This vow forbids defilement even for parents and siblings, whereas ordinary priests receive explicit exceptions for those close relatives.

Numbers 6:8
All the days of his vow he shall be holy unto the Lord.
  Numbers 6:5 — Same-chapter consecration definition: The declaration that the vowed person is holy throughout the vow restates the holiness attached to the period of uncut consecrated hair.
  Judges 13:7 — Consecration duration contrast: The law's holiness lasts for the days of a vow, whereas Samson is announced as God's Nazirite from the womb until death.

Numbers 6:9
But if anyone dies suddenly beside him, the head of his vow shall be defiled forthwith. He shall shave his head on the day when he is cleansed; on the seventh day it shall be shaved.
  Numbers 19:11-12 — Corpse purification parallel: Sudden death beside the vowed person requires seventh-day cleansing, corresponding to the seven-day impurity and seventh-day purification after corpse contact.
  Leviticus 14:9 — Purification procedure parallel: The vowed person shaves on the seventh day of cleansing, as the person cleansed from skin disease also shaves on the seventh day.

Numbers 6:10
And on the eighth day he shall bring two turtledoves or two young pigeons unto the priest, at the doors of the Tent of Testimony.
  Leviticus 15:14 — Ritual procedure parallel: Both purification procedures require two turtledoves or two young pigeons brought on the eighth day to the priest at the Tent entrance.
  Leviticus 14:22 — Offering parallel: The two alternative pairs of birds prescribed for the defiled vow also occur among the offerings for a poor person being cleansed.

Numbers 6:11
And the priest shall prepare one for sin and one for a whole burnt offering. And the priest shall make atonement for him concerning his sin in connection with the dead person, and shall consecrate his head on that day,
  Leviticus 15:15 — Ritual procedure parallel: In both passages the priest prepares one bird for sin and one as a whole burnt offering and makes atonement for the person.
  Leviticus 5:7-10 — Offering procedure parallel: The paired sin and whole burnt bird offerings and the priest's atonement follow the same differentiated offering pattern described in Leviticus.

Numbers 6:12
whereon he was consecrated unto the Lord for the days of the vow. And he shall bring a year-old lamb for a guilt offering; and the former days shall not be reckoned, for the head of his vow was defiled.
  Numbers 6:9 — Same-chapter legal consequence: The loss of previously counted vow days is the stated consequence of the sudden corpse defilement and damaged consecration described earlier.
  Leviticus 14:21 — Guilt offering parallel: The renewed vow requires a lamb for guilt; the poor person's cleansing likewise includes a lamb for offence and atonement, despite different circumstances.

Numbers 6:13
And this is the law of him who has vowed: on the day when he fulfils the days of his vow, he shall present his offering at the doors of the Tent of Testimony,
  Acts 21:26 — Vow completion parallel: The law connects completion of the vowed days with presenting an offering; Acts likewise speaks of completed purification days and an offering for each man.
  Deuteronomy 23:22-24 — Vow fulfilment: Presenting the offering when the vowed period ends gives concrete expression to Deuteronomy's requirement to fulfil a gift vowed to the Lord.

Numbers 6:14
and he shall bring his gift unto the Lord: one unblemished year-old male lamb for whole burning, and one unblemished year-old ewe lamb for sin, and one unblemished ram for deliverance,
  Leviticus 22:18-21 — Votive offering requirements: The completion offerings must be unblemished, matching Leviticus's requirement that vowed whole burnt and deliverance offerings have no blemish.
  Leviticus 1:10 — Whole burnt offering law: The unblemished male lamb for whole burning belongs to the flock offering category for which Leviticus also requires an unblemished male.

Numbers 6:15
and a basket of unleavened bread of fine flour, loaves prepared with oil, and unleavened wafers spread with oil, and their sacrifice and their drink offering.
  Leviticus 2:4 — Exact bread preparation parallel: The basket's unleavened fine-flour loaves mixed with oil and wafers spread with oil repeat the specified forms of the oven-baked offering.
  Leviticus 7:12 — Bread and deliverance offering: The completion basket accompanies deliverance sacrifice and contains the same oil-prepared loaves and oil-spread unleavened wafers named for thanksgiving sacrifice.

Numbers 6:16
And the priest shall present them before the Lord, and prepare his offering for sin and his whole burnt offering.
  Leviticus 14:19-20 — Offering sequence parallel: The priest prepares sin and whole burnt offerings for the completed vow, as the cleansing rite similarly joins these offerings and priestly atonement.

Numbers 6:17
And he shall prepare the ram as a sacrifice of deliverance unto the Lord, with the basket of unleavened bread; and the priest shall prepare its sacrifice and its drink offering.
  Leviticus 7:11-12 — Deliverance offering accompaniment: The vow's ram of deliverance is offered with unleavened bread, corresponding to the deliverance law's provision of bread prepared with oil.
  Leviticus 7:16 — Votive deliverance offering: The ram is the vowed person's deliverance sacrifice; Leviticus explicitly recognizes a deliverance gift sacrificed as a vow.

Numbers 6:18
And he who has vowed shall shave the head of his vow at the doors of the Tent of Testimony, and put the hair upon the fire which is under the sacrifice of deliverance.
  Acts 21:24 — Vow completion parallel: The legal shaving of the consecrated head corresponds to the vowed men whose expenses Paul is asked to pay so they may shave their heads.
  Acts 18:18 — Vow and haircut association: The law associates the end of consecration with shaving, and Acts expressly associates Paul's haircut at Cenchreae with a vow, without describing the full rite.

Numbers 6:19
And the priest shall take the boiled foreleg from the ram, and one unleavened loaf from the basket, and one unleavened wafer, and put them upon the hands of him who has vowed, after he has shaved the hair of his vow.
  Leviticus 8:26-27 — Consecration presentation parallel: The ram portion, one loaf, and one wafer placed on the vowed person's hands resemble the consecration portions placed on Aaron's and his sons' hands.
  Leviticus 7:32-34 — Priestly portion background: The ram's foreleg is presented in the completed vow, while the deliverance law identifies the foreleg and breast as portions assigned to the priest.

Numbers 6:20
And the priest shall bring them as a presentation before the Lord. It shall be holy unto the priest, with the breast of presentation and the foreleg of the portion set apart; and afterward he who has vowed shall drink wine.
  Leviticus 7:34 — Priestly portion parallel: The breast of presentation and foreleg set apart are holy to the priest here, precisely the portions Leviticus grants to Aaron and his sons.
  Numbers 6:3-4 — Same-chapter restriction and release: Permission to drink wine after presentation marks the termination of the earlier vow restrictions on wine and every product of the vine.

Numbers 6:21
This is the law of him who vows unto the Lord, his gift unto the Lord concerning the vow, apart from whatever his hand may find, according to the extent of his vow which he vows under the law of consecration.
  Deuteronomy 23:22-24 — Vow fulfilment: The concluding law ties the gift to the extent of the person's vow, while Deuteronomy requires performance of the gift actually promised by the lips.
  Ecclesiastes 5:3-4 — Vow fulfilment: The law measures the offering by the vow undertaken, and Ecclesiastes correspondingly insists that a person pay whatever has been vowed to God.

Numbers 6:22
And the Lord spoke unto Moses, saying:
  Numbers 6:23-26 — Same-chapter speech introduction: This speech introduces Aaron and his sons' specific blessing of Israel, naming divine protection, mercy, the shining face, and peace.

Numbers 6:23
Speak unto Aaron and unto his sons, saying: Thus you shall bless the sons of Israel, saying unto them (and they shall put My name upon the sons of Israel, and I, the Lord, will bless them):
  Deuteronomy 10:8 — Priestly blessing commission: Aaron's sons are told to bless Israel with God's name, corresponding to Levi's appointed ministry of blessing in the Lord's name.
  Leviticus 9:22-23 — Priestly blessing enacted: The instruction that Aaron and his sons bless Israel is enacted when Aaron raises his hands and Moses and Aaron bless the assembled people.
  Deuteronomy 21:5 — Priestly blessing commission: The priests' command to place the divine name upon Israel matches the stated Levitical appointment to stand before God and bless in His name.

Numbers 6:24
The Lord bless you and keep you;
  Psalms 120:5-8 — Blessing echoed: The blessing asks the Lord to keep the hearer; the psalm expands that promise by repeatedly naming the Lord as guardian of life and journeys.
  Psalms 66:2 — Blessing echoed: The psalm's plea that God have compassion and bless His people repeats the priestly blessing's appeal for divine blessing and favor.

Numbers 6:25
the Lord make His face shine upon you, and have mercy upon you;
  Psalms 66:2 — Blessing echoed: The prayer that God's face shine upon the people closely repeats the priestly petition for His shining face and mercy.
  Psalms 30:17 — Blessing echoed: Both passages ask God to make His face shine upon the recipient and connect that favor explicitly with His mercy.
  Psalms 79:4 — Blessing echoed: The psalm seeks salvation through God's shining face, reusing the priestly blessing's particular image of favorable divine illumination.

Numbers 6:26
the Lord lift up His face upon you, and give you peace.
  Psalms 28:11 — Blessing echoed: The priestly blessing culminates in God granting peace; the psalm expressly says the Lord will bless His people with peace.
  John 14:27 — Gift of peace parallel: The blessing asks the Lord to give peace, and Jesus explicitly gives His peace to His disciples while reassuring their troubled hearts.

Numbers 7:1
And it came to pass, on the day when Moses finished setting up the Tent, that he anointed it and sanctified it and all its vessels, and the altar and all its vessels; and he anointed them and sanctified them,
  Exodus 40:9-10 — Command and fulfilment: Moses' anointing and sanctifying of the completed Tent, utensils, and altar fulfils the detailed command to anoint and sanctify those objects.
  Leviticus 8:10-11 — Same consecration event: Both accounts describe Moses using anointing oil to consecrate the Tent, altar, and all their utensils at the sanctuary's inauguration.

Numbers 7:2
and the rulers of Israel presented offerings, twelve rulers of their ancestral houses; these are the rulers of the tribes, these are they who stood over the enrolment.
  Numbers 1:4-16 — Named leaders identified: The twelve ancestral rulers who present the gifts are identified as those over enrolment, corresponding to the named tribal rulers appointed for that census.
  Numbers 7:12-83 — Same-chapter collective and details: The general notice of twelve ruling donors is unfolded in the twelve successive named tribal presentations, each with its own day and gifts.

Numbers 7:3
And they brought their gifts before the Lord: six covered wagons and twelve oxen, a wagon from every two rulers and a calf from each. They presented them before the Tent.
  Numbers 7:7-8 — Same-chapter gift allocation: The initial six wagons and twelve oxen are accounted for by the later allocation of two and four wagons with four and eight oxen.

Numbers 7:4
And the Lord said unto Moses, saying:
  Numbers 7:5-9 — Same-chapter speech introduction: This speech specifically directs Moses to accept the wagons and oxen for Levitical service, followed by allocations suited to each division's ministry.

Numbers 7:5

  Numbers 4:24-28 — Service provision: Assigning transport according to ministry corresponds to Gershon's specified carrying duties for curtains, coverings, hangings, and related utensils.
  Numbers 4:29-33 — Service provision: Giving transport according to ministry also corresponds to Merari's distinct responsibility for the Tent's structural supports, pillars, bases, pegs, and cords.

Numbers 7:6

  Numbers 7:3 — Same-chapter gift and acceptance: Moses receives and distributes to the Levites the same wagons and oxen that the twelve rulers have brought before the Tent.

Numbers 7:7

  Numbers 4:24-28 — Transport and assigned burden: Gershon's two wagons and four oxen support the previously defined carrying ministry of curtains and coverings under Ithamar's oversight.

Numbers 7:8

  Numbers 4:29-33 — Transport and assigned burden: Merari's four wagons and eight oxen support its structural carrying duties, and both passages name Ithamar as supervisor.

Numbers 7:9
And unto the sons of Kohath he gave none, for they have the ministries of the sanctuary; they shall bear them upon their shoulders.
  Numbers 4:4-15 — Transport restriction explained: Kohath receives no wagons because its holy objects must be carried; the earlier instructions prepare those objects with carrying poles for Kohath's ministry.
  1 Chronicles 15:15 — Later observance of transport law: The requirement that sanctuary objects be borne on shoulders is reflected when the Levites carry God's ark with poles as Moses commanded.

Numbers 7:10
And the rulers presented gifts for the dedication of the altar on the day he anointed it. The rulers presented their gifts before the altar.
  1 Kings 8:62-64 — Sanctuary dedication parallel: The rulers dedicate the anointed altar with offerings, as Solomon and Israel later dedicate the Lord's house with large sacrificial offerings.
  Numbers 7:84-88 — Same-chapter dedication summary: The rulers' opening dedication notice is recapitulated by the final inventory of vessels and animals explicitly called the altar's dedication.

Numbers 7:11

  Numbers 7:12-18 — Same-chapter command and observance: The instruction to present one ruler's gift each day begins to be carried out by Nahshon on the first day and Nethanel on the second.
  Numbers 7:72-83 — Same-chapter command and completion: The daily sequence commanded here reaches its eleventh and twelfth days with Pagiel and Ahira, completing the twelve rulers' presentations.

Numbers 7:12

  Numbers 1:7 — Named leader identified: The first-day donor Nahshon son of Amminadab, ruler of Judah, is the same named representative assigned to that tribe in the census list.
  Numbers 2:3 — Named leader identified: Nahshon son of Amminadab leads the Judah presentation on the first day and is also named as that tribe's ruler in the camp arrangement.

Numbers 7:13

  Numbers 7:85 — Same-chapter individual and aggregate: On the first day, Nahshon son of Amminadab presents silver vessels and flour. The silver dish of 130 shekels and bowl of 70 shekels contribute 200 shekels to the final 2,400-shekel total of twelve matched pairs.
  Leviticus 2:1 — Offering preparation parallel: The fine flour prepared with oil in Nahshon's silver vessels has the same essential flour-and-oil preparation prescribed for a grain gift.

Numbers 7:14
one incense vessel of ten gold units, full of incense;
  Numbers 7:86 — Same-chapter individual and aggregate: On the first day, Nahshon son of Amminadab presents a gold incense vessel. This ten-unit gold vessel full of incense is one of the twelve vessels whose combined gold is finally counted as 120 units.

Numbers 7:15
one calf, one ram, and one year-old male lamb for a whole burnt offering;
  Numbers 7:87 — Same-chapter individual and aggregate: On the first day, Nahshon son of Amminadab presents whole burnt animals. The calf, ram, and year-old male lamb for whole burning each contribute one to the closing totals of twelve animals of each kind.

Numbers 7:16
and one male goat from the goats for sin;

  Numbers 7:13 — Same-chapter repeated dedication gift: Elizur son of Shedeur's fourth-day gift matches the first-day presentation by Nahshon. The silver dish and bowl repeat Nahshon's exact 130- and 70-shekel weights and their shared contents of fine flour prepared with oil.

Numbers 7:32
one incense vessel of ten gold units, full of incense;
  Numbers 7:86 — Same-chapter individual and aggregate: On the fourth day, Elizur son of Shedeur presents a gold incense vessel. This ten-unit gold vessel full of incense is one of the twelve vessels whose combined gold is finally counted as 120 units.
  Numbers 7:14 — Same-chapter repeated dedication gift: Elizur son of Shedeur's fourth-day gift matches the first-day presentation by Nahshon. The single gold incense vessel full of incense repeats the first donor's exact vessel, material, contents, and ten-unit weight.

Numbers 7:33
one calf, one ram, and one year-old male lamb for a whole burnt offering;
  Numbers 7:87 — Same-chapter individual and aggregate: On the fourth day, Elizur son of Shedeur presents whole burnt animals. The calf, ram, and year-old male lamb for whole burning each contribute one to the closing totals of twelve animals of each kind.
  Numbers 7:15 — Same-chapter repeated dedication gift: Elizur son of Shedeur's fourth-day gift matches the first-day presentation by Nahshon. This donor's whole burnt offering repeats Nahshon's exact three animals: one calf, one ram, and one year-old male lamb.

Numbers 7:34
and one male goat from the goats for sin;
  Numbers 7:87 — Same-chapter individual and aggregate: On the fourth day, Elizur son of Shedeur presents a sin offering goat. The single male goat for sin is one of the twelve male goats counted for sin in the dedication's final aggregate.
  Numbers 7:16 — Same-chapter repeated dedication gift: Elizur son of Shedeur's fourth-day gift matches the first-day presentation by Nahshon. This donor's single male goat for sin repeats the precise animal, sex, number, and purpose of Nahshon's first-day sin offering.

Numbers 7:35
and for a sacrifice of deliverance: two heifers, five rams, five male goats, and five year-old ewe lambs. This was the gift of Elizur son of Shedeur.
  Numbers 7:88 — Same-chapter individual and aggregate: On the fourth day, Elizur son of Shedeur presents deliverance animals. The two heifers and five each of rams, male goats, and year-old ewe lambs contribute to the final totals of 24 and 60 each.
  Numbers 7:17 — Same-chapter repeated dedication gift: Elizur son of Shedeur's fourth-day gift matches the first-day presentation by Nahshon. This deliverance offering repeats the first donor's two heifers and five each of rams, male goats, and year-old ewe lambs.

  Exodus 33:11 — Earlier account of direct speech with Moses: Exodus describes the Lord speaking with Moses face to face like a friend; this answer describes mouth-to-mouth communication and visible appearance, preserving each passage’s own language about his distinct access.
  Exodus 33:18-23 — Related glory encounter with its stated visual limits: Moses earlier asks to see the Lord’s glory and receives a particular encounter with restrictions upon seeing His face; this verse says he saw the Lord’s glory without erasing those separately stated limits.

Numbers 12:9
And the Lord’s fierce anger was upon them, and He departed.
  Numbers 12:8 — Same-chapter anger follows the explicit rebuke: The Lord departs in fierce anger after asking why Aaron and Miriam were unafraid to speak against Moses; the preceding rebuke supplies the particular dispute surrounding this reaction.

Numbers 12:10
And the cloud withdrew from the tent, and behold, Miriam was leprous as snow. Aaron looked at Miriam, and behold, she was leprous.
  Deuteronomy 24:8-9 — Later leprosy instruction expressly recalls Miriam: Deuteronomy joins careful observance of priestly leprosy instructions with remembering what the Lord did to Miriam on the way from Egypt, explicitly recalling this named affliction.
  Exodus 4:6-7 — Related snow-like bodily alteration with different recipients and outcomes: Moses’ hand earlier becomes like snow and is restored as a sign; Miriam here is explicitly called leprous as snow, preserving the different recipients, occasions, and terms rather than importing a diagnosis into Exodus.

Numbers 12:11
And Aaron said unto Moses: I beseech you, my lord, do not lay sin upon us, for we acted ignorantly in that we sinned.
  Numbers 12:1-2 — Same-chapter confession follows the particular family criticism: Aaron’s acknowledgment that they sinned follows his and Miriam’s criticism of Moses’ marriage and challenge to his distinctive reception of divine speech, identifying the preceding acts rather than inventing another offence.

Numbers 12:12
Let her not be as one equal to death, as a miscarried child coming out of a mother’s womb; and it consumes half her flesh.
  Numbers 12:10 — Same-chapter plea depicts the already reported affliction: Aaron’s plea compares Miriam’s condition to death and a partly consumed miscarried child; the preceding report identifies the affliction that he saw, leprosy as snow, without requiring a medical interpretation of his simile.

Numbers 12:13
And Moses cried unto the Lord, saying: O God, I beseech Thee, heal her.
  Numbers 12:11-12 — Same-chapter healing petition answers Aaron’s specific appeal: Aaron asks that their sin not be charged and that Miriam not remain like one near death; Moses responds with the direct plea that God heal her.
  Numbers 11:2 — Related intercession by Moses during an affliction: Moses earlier prays after the people appeal to him during the camp fire, which then subsides; here he cries for Miriam’s healing, retaining the different affliction and subsequent seven-day exclusion.

Numbers 12:14
And the Lord said unto Moses: If her father had spat in her face, would she not be ashamed for seven days? Let her be set apart outside the camp for seven days; afterward she shall enter.
  Leviticus 13:45-46 — Leprosy exclusion rule and Miriam’s specified interval: Leviticus requires a person with the lesion to dwell apart outside camp while unclean; this ruling specifically sets Miriam outside for seven days and then permits her return.
  Numbers 12:15 — Same-chapter seven-day exclusion order carried out: The Lord orders Miriam set apart outside the camp for seven days before entering; the next report records that interval and the people waiting until she was cleansed.

Numbers 12:15
And Miriam was set apart outside the camp for seven days, and the people did not set out until Miriam was cleansed.
  Numbers 12:14 — Same-chapter reported exclusion follows its specific order: Miriam’s seven days outside camp carry out the immediately preceding order; the report additionally states that the people did not depart until she was cleansed.
  Leviticus 13:46 — Miriam’s outside-camp separation and the general lesion rule: The general rule requires an unclean person with the lesion to live apart outside the camp; Miriam’s reported separation applies that location while retaining its specifically ordered seven days.

Numbers 12:16
And afterward the people set out from Hazeroth and camped in the wilderness of Paran.
  Numbers 11:35 — Narrative itinerary joins arrival at and departure from Hazeroth: The earlier notice brings the people from Graves of Craving to Hazeroth; this notice resumes their departure from Hazeroth after Miriam’s exclusion and names Paran as the next wilderness.
  Numbers 13:3 — Paran camp and the following scout departure: Israel camps in the wilderness of Paran here; Moses subsequently sends the tribal leaders from that named wilderness to explore the land, connecting the stated location across the episodes.

Numbers 13:1
And the Lord spoke unto Moses, saying:
  Numbers 13:2-3 — Same-chapter speech introduction and particular scouting commission: This speech introduces the specific command to send tribal leaders to search Canaan and the report that Moses sends them from Paran through the Lord’s voice.

Numbers 13:2
Send men for yourself, and let them search out the land of the Canaanites, which I give unto the sons of Israel for a possession. You shall send one man from each tribe, according to the groups of their ancestral families, each a leader among them.
  Deuteronomy 1:22-23 — Related account of selecting one scout per tribe: Deuteronomy recalls the people’s request to explore the land and Moses taking twelve men, one from each tribe; this command gives the divine commission and requires each tribal representative to be a leader.
  Genesis 12:6-7 — Earlier promise of Canaanite land to Abram’s descendants: The earlier scene locates Abram where Canaanites live and promises that land to his seed; this scouting commission identifies Canaanite land as the possession the Lord gives Israel.

Numbers 13:3
And Moses sent them from the wilderness of Paran through the Lord’s voice. All these men were leaders of the sons of Israel.
  Numbers 12:16 — Scouts sent from the wilderness where Israel has just camped: The preceding itinerary places Israel in the wilderness of Paran; this report names that same wilderness as the point from which Moses sends the tribal leaders.
  Deuteronomy 1:23 — Related execution of the tribal scout selection: Moses later recalls taking twelve men, one per tribe, for the exploration; this sending account identifies the selected men as Israelite leaders and explicitly locates their departure in Paran.

Numbers 13:16

  Numbers 14:6 — Renamed Joshua appears among the returning faithful scouts: The renaming explicitly identifies Joshua as Hoshea son of Nun; the following episode names Joshua son of Nun and Caleb among the explorers, carrying that supplied identity into the reported response.

Numbers 13:17
And Moses sent them to search out the land of Canaan, and said unto them: Go up by this wilderness, and you shall go up into the hill country;
  Deuteronomy 1:24 — Related ascent into the hill country for exploration: The later recollection describes the scouts turning and going up into the hill country; this instruction tells them to go up through the wilderness into that terrain before exploring.

Numbers 13:18
and you shall see the land, what it is, and the people settled upon it, whether they are stronger or weak, whether they are few or many;
  Numbers 13:28 — Same-chapter inquiry into population strength and the report: Moses asks whether the inhabitants are strong or weak and few or many; the returning report describes the nation as bold and adds its fortifications and Anak family observation.

Numbers 13:19
And is the land on which they are settled good or bad? What are the towns in which they live—walled or unwalled?
  Numbers 13:27-28 — Same-chapter questions about land and towns answered in the report: The inspection asks whether the land is good and the towns walled; the report describes milk and honey and fruit, but also very large, strongly fortified cities.

Numbers 13:20
And what is the land like—rich or neglected? Are there trees therein or not? And persevere, and you shall take of the fruits of the land. And the days were days of spring, forerunners of the grape harvest.
  Numbers 13:23 — Same-chapter instruction to take fruit and the gathered specimens: Moses tells the scouts to take the land’s fruit; they later cut and carry a grape cluster with pomegranates and figs, supplying the particular specimens resulting from that instruction.

Numbers 13:21
And they went up and inspected the land from the wilderness of Sin to Raab, as one enters Ephaath.
  Numbers 13:2-3 — Same-chapter reported inspection executes the commission: The inspection from Sin to Raab toward Ephaath is the scouts’ execution of the command to search Canaan; this comparison preserves the reported endpoints without substituting other familiar place spellings.

Numbers 13:22
And they went up through the wilderness and came as far as Hebron, where Ahiman, Sheshai, and Talmai, families of Anak, were. Hebron had been built seven years before Tanis in Egypt.
  Joshua 14:12-14 — Later Hebron inheritance and its Anakim setting: Caleb later recalls the Anakim and fortified cities and receives Hebron as his inheritance; this earlier exploration places Anak’s families at Hebron, without requiring equivalence between differently rendered individual names elsewhere.

Numbers 13:23
And they came as far as the Ravine of the Cluster and inspected it. They cut from there a branch with one cluster of grapes upon it and carried it on carrying poles, with some pomegranates and figs.
  Deuteronomy 1:24-25 — Later recollection of the cluster valley and carried fruit: Moses later recalls the scouts reaching the Valley of the Cluster, exploring, and bringing down fruit; this account gives the carried grape branch, pomegranates, and figs in detail.
  Deuteronomy 8:8 — Fruit samples and the later good-land produce list: The carried grapes, pomegranates, and figs correspond to vines, fig trees, and pomegranates in the later land description, while its broader grain and oil inventory remains distinct.

Numbers 13:24

  Numbers 13:23 — Same-chapter place-name explained by the cut cluster: The Ravine of the Cluster is named for the cluster cut there; the preceding account describes that particular branch and its transport with pomegranates and figs.

Numbers 13:25
And they returned from there after inspecting the land for forty days.
  Numbers 14:34 — Later judgment expressly uses the forty scouting days: The judgment explicitly bases forty years upon the forty days of exploration, a day for a year, reusing the expedition duration reported here without changing either unit.

Numbers 13:26
And they came unto Moses, Aaron, and the whole congregation of the sons of Israel in the wilderness of Paran, Kadesh. They gave a report to them and the whole congregation and showed the land’s fruit.
  Deuteronomy 1:24-25 — Returning scouts’ report and displayed fruit recalled later: The later recollection likewise describes bringing fruit from the inspected land and reporting it good; this scene identifies the full receiving assembly and the Paran, Kadesh setting.

Numbers 13:27
And they told him, and said: We came unto the land to which you sent us, a land flowing with milk and honey; and this is its fruit.
  Deuteronomy 1:25 — Corresponding positive report accompanied by land fruit: The later recollection says the explorers brought fruit and reported the land good; this report displays its fruit and calls it a land flowing with milk and honey.
  Numbers 14:7-8 — Positive land description repeated by Joshua and Caleb: Joshua and Caleb subsequently call the inspected land exceedingly good and flowing with milk and honey; their appeal retains the positive description before arguing that the Lord can bring Israel into it.

Numbers 13:28
But the nation living on it is bold, and the cities are strongly fortified with walls and very large. We saw the family of Anak there.
  Deuteronomy 1:28 — Later recollection of the discouraging fortification and giant report: Moses later recounts the people’s fear from their brothers’ report of a stronger numerous nation, great walled cities, and giants, corresponding to this report’s bold inhabitants, fortifications, and Anak family.
  Deuteronomy 9:1-3 — Later acknowledgment of strong cities and the sons of Anak: Deuteronomy again acknowledges mighty nations, great walls, and Anak’s tall people, yet promises the Lord’s action; this scout report supplies the earlier observation without that later reassurance.

Numbers 13:29
And Amalek dwells in the land toward the south; and the Hittite, and the Hivite, and the Jebusite, and the Amorite dwell in the hill country; and the Canaanite dwells beside the sea and beside the river Jordan.
  Deuteronomy 7:1 — Related inhabitants of the promised land with nonidentical lists: Both passages name Hittites, Amorites, Canaanites, Hivites, and Jebusites; this geographic report also names Amalek, whereas Deuteronomy’s seven-nation list includes Girgashites and Perizzites, so their lists are not treated as identical.

Numbers 13:30
And Caleb quieted the people before Moses and said to him: No! We shall certainly go up and inherit it, for we shall surely prevail against them.
  Numbers 14:6-9 — Caleb’s confidence developed in the joint faithful appeal: Caleb here urges ascent and confidence of prevailing; he and Joshua subsequently affirm the land’s goodness and the Lord’s presence and tell Israel not to fear its inhabitants.
  Joshua 14:7-9 — Caleb later recalls his report and fidelity: Caleb later distinguishes his own report and following the Lord from companions who turned the people’s heart, recalling the stance expressed here when he urges Israel to inherit the land.

Numbers 13:31
And the men who had gone up with him said: We are not going up, for we shall certainly not be able to go up against the nation, because it is much stronger than we are.
  Numbers 14:1-4 — Discouraging scouting claim followed by the congregation’s reaction: The companions deny Israel’s ability to ascend against the stronger nation; the next scene reports widespread weeping, complaints, and a plan to return to Egypt, showing the specific narrative aftermath.
  Deuteronomy 1:26-28 — Later recollection of refusal and the stronger-nation report: Deuteronomy recalls refusal to ascend and the discouraging account of a greater, stronger nation and fortified cities, matching the inability and relative-strength claim made by these explorers.

Numbers 13:32
And they brought dismay concerning the land which they had searched out unto the sons of Israel, saying: The land through which we passed to search it out is a land that devours its inhabitants; all the people whom we saw therein are men of exceeding height;
  Numbers 14:36-37 — Later judgment expressly identifies the evil scouting report: The following judgment describes men sent by Moses returning with evil words about the land and dying in a plague, supplying the stated consequence of the dismay-producing report introduced here.

Numbers 13:33
And there we saw the giants. We were like grasshoppers before them, and indeed so we were before them.
  Deuteronomy 1:28 — Later recollection of the reported giants: The later account recalls the brothers reporting giants in the land and the people’s consequent fear; this report adds the scouts’ grasshopper comparison, which the target does not separately repeat.

Numbers 14:1
And the whole congregation raised its voice, and the people wept all that night.
  Numbers 13:31-33 — Reaction to the discouraging report: The congregation's night of weeping follows the explorers' claim that the land's inhabitants are too strong and terrifying.

Numbers 14:2
And all the sons of Israel grumbled against Moses and Aaron. The whole congregation said to them: If only we had died in the land of Egypt! Or if only we had died in this wilderness!
  Deuteronomy 1:26-28 — Later recollection of refusal: Both accounts describe Israel's refusal and complaints against the exodus after hearing a frightening report about the land.
  Numbers 14:28-29 — Same-chapter complaint and answered judgment: The wish to have died in the wilderness is answered by the announced wilderness deaths of the registered grumblers.

Numbers 14:3
And why is the Lord bringing us into this land to fall in war? Our wives and children will become plunder. So now it is better for us to return to Egypt.
  Numbers 14:31 — Same-chapter fear and reversed outcome: The children feared to become plunder are expressly promised entry and inheritance in the later judgment.
  Deuteronomy 1:27 — Parallel accusation concerning the exodus: Both complaints interpret the departure from Egypt as bringing Israel into danger of destruction by the land's inhabitants.

Numbers 14:4
And they said to one another, Let us appoint a leader and return to Egypt.
  Nehemiah 9:17 — Historical recollection: The proposal to appoint a leader and return to Egypt is recalled as appointing leadership to return to Egyptian slavery.

Numbers 14:5
And Moses and Aaron fell on their faces before the whole congregation of the sons of Israel.
  Numbers 16:22 — Intercessory posture in congregation crises: Moses and Aaron fall on their faces before the rebellious congregation here; they later fall together and plead against collective destruction.

Numbers 14:6
Joshua son of Nun and Caleb son of Jephunneh, among those who had explored the land, tore their garments.
  Numbers 13:6 — Caleb identified among the explorers: Caleb son of Jephunneh, who tears his garments with Joshua, is the named Judah representative in the exploration list.
  Numbers 13:16 — Joshua identified among the explorers: The exploration list explains that Moses named Hoshea son of Nun Joshua, identifying the companion who protests with Caleb.

Numbers 14:7
And they said to the whole congregation of the sons of Israel: The land we inspected is exceedingly, exceedingly good.
  Deuteronomy 1:25 — Land's goodness affirmed: The explorers' affirmation that the land is exceedingly good corresponds to the later recollection of its good produce and report.
  Numbers 13:27 — Report of the land's abundance: Joshua and Caleb affirm the goodness of the inspected land whose milk, honey, and fruit were already reported.

Numbers 14:8
If the Lord chooses us, He will bring us into this land and give it to us, a land flowing with milk and honey.
  Exodus 33:1-3 — Promised land and divine presence: Both passages speak of God's bringing Israel into a land flowing with milk and honey, while distinguishing the issue of His accompanying presence.
  Deuteronomy 1:21 — Encouragement to receive the promised land: Both exhortations ground entry and inheritance in the Lord's gift of the land rather than the inhabitants' apparent strength.

Numbers 14:9
Only do not become deserters from the Lord. Do not fear the people of the land, for they are food for us. Their season has passed away from them, but the Lord is among us. Do not fear them.
  Deuteronomy 1:29-30 — Divine presence answers fear: Both exhortations forbid fear of the inhabitants because the Lord goes with Israel and acts against its enemies.
  Numbers 14:42-43 — Same-chapter contrasting presence and absence: The appeal not to fear because the Lord is among them contrasts with the later warning that disobedient advance lacks His presence.

Numbers 14:10

  Numbers 16:19 — Glory appears during hostile assembly: In both confrontations a hostile congregation gathers against God's servants and the Lord's glory appears at the Tent.

Numbers 14:11

  Deuteronomy 9:23 — Refusal characterized as unbelief: The Lord's charge of refusing belief despite His signs corresponds to the later description of Kadesh disobedience and unbelief.
  Hebrews 3:16-19 — Wilderness unbelief interpreted: Hebrews likewise links the exodus generation's provocation and disobedience with unbelief that prevented entry.

Numbers 14:12

  Exodus 32:10 — Earlier threat and replacement nation: Both divine speeches threaten destruction of the rebellious people and promise to make Moses into a great nation.

Numbers 14:13

  Exodus 32:11-12 — Intercession appeals to the exodus: Moses again appeals to God's powerful rescue from Egypt and what the Egyptians would say about destroying the rescued people.

Numbers 14:14

  Exodus 13:21-22 — Cloud and fire recalled: Moses' description of guidance by cloud in daytime and fire at night recalls the explicitly narrated pillars of the exodus.
  Deuteronomy 1:33 — Guidance recalled in the same crisis: Both passages describe the Lord going before Israel in cloud by day and fire at night in the context of unbelief.

Numbers 14:15

  Deuteronomy 9:28 — Intercession anticipates foreign interpretation: The prospect of nations explaining Israel's destruction is repeated in the later plea against a false conclusion about God's ability.

Numbers 14:16
Because the Lord was unable to bring this people into the land He swore to them, He laid them low in the wilderness.
  Deuteronomy 9:28 — Same argument about divine ability: Both intercessions anticipate the claim that God killed Israel in the wilderness because He could not bring them into the promised land.

Numbers 14:17
And now let Thy strength be exalted, O Lord, as Thou hast spoken, saying:
  Numbers 14:18 — Same-chapter appeal introduces recalled words: The appeal to what the Lord has spoken introduces the following declaration of patience, mercy, forgiveness, and judgment.

Numbers 14:18
The Lord is long-suffering, and abundant in mercy, and true, taking away lawlessness and wrongdoing and sins; yet He will in no wise cleanse the guilty, repaying the sins of fathers upon children unto the third and fourth generations.
  Exodus 34:6-7 — Recalled divine self-declaration: Moses recalls the Lord's patience, abundant mercy, truth, forgiveness, and judgment extending to the third and fourth generations.

Numbers 14:19

  Exodus 34:9 — Intercession seeks forgiveness and continued relationship: Both prayers ask God to remove the sins of the people in connection with His gracious presence among them.
  Numbers 14:20 — Same-chapter petition and response: Moses asks forgiveness according to great mercy, and the Lord answers that He is gracious according to Moses' word.

Numbers 14:20

  Exodus 32:14 — Merciful response to Moses' intercession: Both accounts record a merciful divine response following Moses' appeal against destruction of the people.

Numbers 14:21
