  Psalms 71:19 — Earth filled with divine glory: The declaration that the Lord's glory will fill the earth parallels the psalm's blessing of His name and universal glory.

Numbers 14:22
all the men who have seen My glory and the signs I did in Egypt and this wilderness, and have tested Me this tenth time and have not listened to My voice,
  Psalms 94:9-11 — Testing despite witnessed works: The psalm likewise recalls ancestors testing God after seeing His works and the oath excluding that generation from rest.

Numbers 14:23
shall certainly not see the land I swore to their fathers. But their children who are here with Me, who do not know good or evil, every inexperienced young person—to these I will give the land. None of those who provoke Me shall see it.
  Deuteronomy 1:35-39 — Denied entry and children's inheritance: Both accounts distinguish the excluded generation from young children who do not know good or evil and will inherit the land.
  Hebrews 3:18-19 — Disobedient generation excluded: Hebrews interprets the exclusion from rest in terms of the disobedience and unbelief that Numbers identifies among those provoking God.

Numbers 14:24
But My servant Caleb, because a different spirit was in him and he followed Me, I will bring into the land he entered, and his seed shall inherit it.
  Deuteronomy 1:36 — Caleb's promised exception: Both passages promise Caleb and his descendants the land because he followed or cleaved to the Lord.
  Joshua 14:9-14 — Caleb's inheritance received: Joshua recalls the promise to Caleb and his children and records Hebron's assignment because Caleb followed the Lord.

Numbers 14:25
Amalek and the Canaanites live in the valley. Tomorrow turn and depart for the wilderness by the way of the Red Sea.
  Deuteronomy 1:40 — Retreat by the Red Sea route: Both accounts instruct the excluded generation to turn back into the wilderness by the way of the Red Sea.

Numbers 14:26
And the Lord spoke unto Moses and Aaron, saying:
  Numbers 14:27-35 — Same-chapter speech introduces specified judgment: This speech introduces the response to grumbling, the twenty-year census threshold, the two exceptions, and the forty-year wilderness judgment.

Numbers 14:27
How long with this evil congregation? They grumble before Me; I have heard the sons of Israel’s grumbling that they have directed against you.
  Numbers 14:2 — Same-chapter complaint heard and judged: The Lord identifies the grumbling directed against Moses and Aaron that the congregation voiced at the chapter's beginning.
  1 Corinthians 10:10 — Warning from wilderness grumbling: Paul warns against grumbling by recalling people who grumbled and were destroyed, matching this judgment's stated concern without identifying one exclusive incident.

Numbers 14:28
Say to them, I live, says the Lord: I will certainly do to you just as you have spoken into My ears.
  Numbers 14:2 — Same-chapter complaint becomes sentence: The Lord's promise to act as they spoke answers their express wish to have died in the wilderness.

Numbers 14:29
In this wilderness your bodies shall fall, with your entire registration and those of you counted from twenty years old and upward who grumbled against Me.
  Numbers 1:2-3 — Registration threshold identifies judged population: The judgment names the registered grumblers from twenty years upward, the threshold used for Israel's military census.
  Hebrews 3:17 — Fallen wilderness bodies recalled: Hebrews explicitly identifies the sinful generation's bodies falling in the wilderness during the forty-year period.

Numbers 14:30
You shall certainly not enter the land over which I stretched out My hand to settle you upon it, except Caleb son of Jephunneh and Joshua son of Nun.
  Numbers 26:64-65 — Later census confirms the exceptions: The later registration reports that none of the earlier registered generation remained except Caleb and Joshua, as the Lord had said.
  Deuteronomy 1:36-38 — Caleb and Joshua excepted: The later recollection separately names Caleb and Joshua as entering the land despite the exclusion pronounced upon the generation.

Numbers 14:31
And I will bring into the land the children whom you said would become plunder, and they shall inherit the land from which you turned away.
  Deuteronomy 1:39 — Children inherit despite adult refusal: Both promises give the land to the younger children, reversing the adults' fears about their fate.
  Numbers 14:3 — Same-chapter feared plunder reversed: The promised inheritance directly answers the earlier complaint that the wives and children would become plunder.

Numbers 14:32
And your bodies shall fall in this wilderness.
  Numbers 26:65 — Wilderness death sentence recalled: The later census explicitly recalls the Lord's declaration that the judged generation would die in the wilderness.

Numbers 14:33
But your sons shall live as herders in the wilderness for forty years and bear your whoredom, until your bodies are consumed in the wilderness.
  Psalms 94:10-11 — Forty-year generation under judgment: The psalm likewise associates the rejected wilderness generation with forty years and exclusion from the Lord's rest.
  Nehemiah 9:21 — Same duration with continued provision: The forty-year wilderness sentence corresponds to the later recollection of God sustaining Israel there throughout forty years, without equating judgment with abandonment.

Numbers 14:34
According to the number of days you searched out the land—forty days, a day for a year—you shall bear your sins for forty years, and you shall know the fury of My anger.
  Numbers 13:25 — Exploration duration determines sentence: The forty days spent inspecting the land supply the explicitly stated day-for-year measure of the forty-year sentence.

Numbers 14:35
I, the Lord, have spoken. I will certainly do this to this evil congregation that has banded together against Me. In this wilderness they shall be consumed, and there they shall die.
  Numbers 26:64-65 — Announced generation judgment fulfilled: The later registration finds the earlier counted generation gone, with only the two named exceptions, recalling this wilderness sentence.

Numbers 14:36
And the men Moses had sent to inspect the land came back and grumbled against it before the congregation, bringing out evil words about the land.
  Numbers 13:31-33 — Cause of the explorers' judgment: The men condemned for bringing evil words are the explorers whose report denied Israel's ability and spread fear about the land.

Numbers 14:37
And those men who spoke evil against the land died in the plague before the Lord.
  Numbers 14:36 — Same-chapter offenders and consequence: The plague's victims are expressly the men just identified as spreading the evil report about the inspected land.

Numbers 14:38
And Joshua son of Nun and Caleb son of Jephunneh lived, out of those men who had gone to explore the land.
  Numbers 14:30 — Same-chapter surviving explorers and named exceptions: Joshua and Caleb survive among the explorers, corresponding to the two men explicitly excepted from the entry prohibition.
  Numbers 26:65 — Survivors identified in later census: The later registration again singles out Caleb and Joshua as the survivors of the generation sentenced to wilderness death.

Numbers 14:39
And Moses spoke these words to all the sons of Israel, and the people mourned greatly.
  Numbers 14:28-35 — Same-chapter judgment communicated and mourned: The people's mourning follows Moses' communication of the stated deaths, exceptions, and forty-year sentence.

Numbers 14:40
And they rose early in the morning and went up to the top of the mountain, saying: Here we are! We will go up to the place the Lord has spoken of, for we have sinned.
  Deuteronomy 1:41 — Unauthorized advance recalled: Both accounts describe Israel confessing sin and proposing to ascend and fight after the judgment had forbidden their entry.

Numbers 14:41
And Moses said: Why do you transgress the Lord’s word? Things will not go well for you.
  Deuteronomy 1:43 — Advance identified as disobedience: Moses identifies the proposed ascent as transgression, and Deuteronomy describes the actual forced advance as rejecting his warning.

Numbers 14:42
Do not go up, for the Lord is not with you, and you shall fall before your enemies.
  Deuteronomy 1:42 — Warning of defeat without divine presence: Both warnings forbid ascent and fighting because the Lord is not with them and they would be crushed by enemies.

Numbers 14:43
Amalek and the Canaanites are there before you, and you shall fall by the sword. Because you turned away in disobedience unto the Lord, the Lord will not be among you.
  Numbers 14:45 — Same-chapter warning and defeat: The warning names Amalek and the Canaanites who subsequently descend from the mountain and rout the advancing people.

Numbers 14:44
And they forced their way up to the top of the mountain. The ark of the Lord’s covenant and Moses did not move from the camp.
  Deuteronomy 1:43 — Forced ascent despite warning: Both accounts describe Israel forcing its way into the hill country after refusing the command not to go.
  Numbers 14:42 — Same-chapter absence signified in the camp: The ark and Moses remaining in camp accompany the earlier warning that the Lord is not with the attempted advance.

Numbers 14:45
And Amalek and the Canaanites who lived on that mountain came down, routed them, and cut them down as far as Erman. Then they returned to the camp.
  Deuteronomy 1:44 — Parallel defeat with distinct particulars: Both accounts report a routed mountain advance, while Numbers names Amalek, Canaanites, and Erman and Deuteronomy names Amorites and its own route.

Numbers 15:1
And the Lord spoke unto Moses, saying:
  Numbers 15:2-5 — Same-chapter speech introduction: This speech introduces offerings upon entering the given land and the specified flour, oil, and drink offering for a lamb.

Numbers 15:2
Speak unto the sons of Israel, and you shall say unto them: When you enter the land of your dwelling, which I give unto you,
  Numbers 14:31 — Promised entry continues beyond judgment: The instruction for entering the given land follows the promise that the children of the judged generation will inherit it.

Numbers 15:3
and you make whole burnt offerings unto the Lord, a whole offering or a sacrifice, to honour a vow, or willingly, or at your feasts, to make a sweet savour unto the Lord, whether from the cattle or from the sheep,
  Leviticus 22:18-21 — Votive and voluntary offering categories: Both laws distinguish vowed or voluntary whole burnt and deliverance offerings from the cattle or flock presented to the Lord.

Numbers 15:4
And the one presenting a gift unto the Lord shall bring a sacrifice of one tenth of an oiphi of fine flour mixed with one quarter of a hin of oil.
  Numbers 28:5 — Identical flour and oil quantities: Both instructions specify one tenth of an oiphi of fine flour mixed with a quarter hin of oil.
  Exodus 29:40 — Continual lamb accompaniment: The tenth measure of fine flour and quarter hin of oil correspond to the accompaniment prescribed for the daily lamb.

Numbers 15:5
And you shall prepare wine for a drink offering, a fourth of a hin, with the whole burning or with the sacrifice; you shall prepare this much for each lamb, an offering of sweet savour unto the Lord.
  Numbers 28:7 — Lamb drink offering measure: Both passages assign a quarter hin as the drink offering for each lamb, while retaining their different stated drink descriptions.
  Exodus 29:40-41 — Wine accompaniment for the lamb: Exodus prescribes a quarter hin of wine with each continual lamb, corresponding to this lamb's wine measure.

Numbers 15:6
And for a ram, when you prepare it for a whole burnt offering or for a sacrifice, you shall prepare a sacrifice of two tenths of fine flour mixed with oil, a third of a hin.
  Numbers 28:12 — Ram's flour quantity: Both offering instructions distinguish the ram by requiring two tenths of fine flour mixed with oil.
  Numbers 15:7 — Same-chapter ram accompaniment completed: The ram's two-tenths flour and third-hin oil prescription is followed by its third-hin wine offering.

Numbers 15:7
And you shall present wine for a drink offering, a third of a hin, for a sweet savour unto the Lord.
  Numbers 28:14 — Ram's drink offering measure: The later calendar offerings preserve the same third hin of wine for each ram specified here.

Numbers 15:8
But if you prepare an animal from the cattle for a whole burnt offering or for a sacrifice to honour a vow, or for a sacrifice of deliverance unto the Lord,
  Leviticus 3:1 — Cattle deliverance offering category: The cattle offering for deliverance belongs to the category Leviticus defines as a cattle sacrifice of deliverance before the Lord.
  Numbers 15:9-10 — Same-chapter cattle category and accompaniment: The introduction of a cattle sacrifice is followed by its distinct three-tenths flour, half-hin oil, and half-hin wine accompaniment.

Numbers 15:9
he shall present with the calf a sacrifice of three tenths of fine flour mixed with oil, half a hin,
  Numbers 28:12 — Calf's flour quantity: Both instructions assign three tenths of fine flour mixed with oil to each calf, distinguishing it from ram and lamb amounts.

Numbers 15:10
and half a hin of wine as a drink offering, an offering of sweet savour unto the Lord.
  Numbers 28:14 — Calf's drink offering measure: The later offering schedule likewise assigns half a hin of wine to each calf.

Numbers 15:11
Thus you shall do for each calf, or each ram, or each lamb from the sheep or from the goats.
  Numbers 15:4-10 — Same-chapter summary of differentiated offerings: The summary directs observance for each animal according to the preceding distinct lamb, ram, and calf accompaniments.

Numbers 15:12
According to the number that you prepare, thus you shall do for each one according to their number.
  Numbers 28:12-14 — Per-animal application of measures: Both sets of instructions apply the prescribed flour and drink quantities to each individual animal rather than one undifferentiated group.

Numbers 15:13
Every native-born person shall do such things in this way when presenting offerings of sweet savour unto the Lord.
  Numbers 15:14-16 — Same-chapter native practice extended to sojourners: The native-born person's manner of presenting offerings becomes the expressly shared standard for attached sojourners in the following rules.

Numbers 15:14
But if a sojourner who joins you in your land, or whosoever is among you throughout your generations, makes an offering of sweet savour unto the Lord, as you do, so shall the congregation do unto the Lord.
  Leviticus 22:18 — Resident foreigners may present offerings: Both laws explicitly include resident foreigners alongside Israel when describing who may bring offerings before the Lord.
  Numbers 15:15-16 — Same-chapter case and governing shared rule: The sojourner's offering case is immediately governed by one law and ordinance for native and attached sojourner.

Numbers 15:15
You shall have one law, both you and the sojourners joined unto you, an everlasting law throughout your generations. As you are, so shall the sojourner be before the Lord.
  Leviticus 24:22 — Equal legal scope with distinct cases: Both passages prescribe one legal standard for native and resident foreigner, though Numbers concerns offerings and Leviticus its stated judgments.

Numbers 15:16
One law and one ordinance shall apply to you and the sojourner attached to you.
  Numbers 15:29 — Same-chapter shared standard in another specified case: The shared ordinance for native and sojourner is repeated specifically for unintentional wrongdoing later in the chapter.

Numbers 15:17
And the Lord spoke unto Moses, saying:
  Numbers 15:18-21 — Same-chapter speech introduction: This speech introduces the specific requirement to set apart the firstfruits of dough when eating the land's bread.

Numbers 15:18
Speak unto the sons of Israel, and you shall say unto them: When you enter the land whither I bring you,
  Numbers 15:19-21 — Same-chapter entry condition and dough offering: Entry into the land establishes the setting for the following command to set apart a portion of its bread and dough.

Numbers 15:19
then it shall be, when you eat of the loaves of the land, that you shall take a portion, a gift set apart unto the Lord.
  Leviticus 23:17-20 — Bread firstfruits compared: Both laws present bread associated with firstfruits before the Lord, while Leviticus specifies its particular festival loaves and accompanying sacrifices.

Numbers 15:20
As the firstfruits of your dough, you shall set apart a loaf for a portion set apart; as a portion from the threshing floor, so you shall take it,
  Romans 11:16 — Firstfruits and the whole lump: The setting apart of dough's firstfruits supplies a particular ritual comparison for Paul's connection between holy firstfruits and the lump.
  Numbers 15:21 — Same-chapter firstfruits instruction restated: The required loaf from the dough's firstfruits is restated as an enduring portion to be given to the Lord.

Numbers 15:21
the firstfruits of your dough; and you shall give unto the Lord a portion set apart throughout your generations.
  Ezekiel 44:30 — First produce for priestly service: Ezekiel likewise assigns firstfruits and first produce to the priests, related to the enduring gift of first produce specified here.

Numbers 15:22
But when you err, and do not carry out all these commandments which the Lord spoke unto Moses,
  Leviticus 4:13 — Congregational unintentional wrongdoing: Both laws introduce wrongdoing in which the congregation fails to observe commandments unintentionally or without recognizing the matter.
  Numbers 15:24-25 — Same-chapter error and required remedy: The introductory case of failing commandments is followed by the specified congregation offerings and atonement when the failure was unintentional.

Numbers 15:23
as the Lord commanded you through Moses, from the day the Lord gave command to you and onward throughout your generations,
  Numbers 15:24 — Same-chapter command scope and unintentional case: The commands transmitted through Moses provide the scope of the following case where the congregation's failure occurs unintentionally.

Numbers 15:24
And if it happened unintentionally, out of the congregation’s sight, the whole congregation shall prepare one unblemished calf from the cattle as a whole burnt offering of sweet savour unto the Lord, with its sacrifice and drink offering according to the ordinance, and one male goat for sin.
  Leviticus 4:13-14 — Congregational offerings with different assignments: Both laws address unnoticed congregational wrongdoing; Numbers prescribes a burnt calf and sin goat, while Leviticus prescribes a calf for sin.
  Numbers 15:25 — Same-chapter prescribed offerings and atonement: The congregation's burnt calf and sin goat are followed by the priest's atonement and forgiveness explicitly conditioned on unintentional action.

Numbers 15:25
And the priest shall make atonement for the whole congregation of the sons of Israel, and they shall be forgiven, because it was unintentional. They have brought their gift, an offering unto the Lord for their sin, before the Lord for their unintentional acts.
  Leviticus 4:20 — Congregational atonement and forgiveness: Both laws explicitly say the priest makes atonement for the congregation and its sin is forgiven.

Numbers 15:26
And forgiveness shall extend throughout the whole congregation of the sons of Israel and to the sojourner attached to you, because it was unintentional for the whole people.
  Numbers 15:29 — Same-chapter sojourner included in forgiveness law: The congregation's unintentional forgiveness includes the sojourner, as the later summary gives native and sojourner one unintentional-offence law.

Numbers 15:27

  Leviticus 4:27-28 — Individual's female goat for unintentional sin: Both laws require a female goat when an individual sins unintentionally, with Numbers additionally specifying its year-old age.

Numbers 15:28
And the priest shall make atonement before the Lord for the person who acted unintentionally and sinned unintentionally, making atonement for that person.
  Leviticus 4:31 — Individual atonement through priestly service: Both individual sin-offering rules require priestly atonement, with Leviticus explicitly adding that the person is forgiven.

Numbers 15:29
For the native among the sons of Israel, and for the sojourner joined unto them, they shall have one law for whosoever acts unintentionally.
  Numbers 15:30 — Same-chapter intent contrasted within shared scope: Both native and sojourner fall under these rules, but the next case distinguishes presumptuous action from the unintentional offences addressed here.

Numbers 15:30
And the soul that acts with a presumptuous hand, whether of the natives or of the sojourners, provokes God; that soul shall be destroyed from his people,
  Numbers 15:27-29 — Same-chapter presumptuous and unintentional acts contrasted: The threat of destruction for presumptuous action differs from the prescribed offering and atonement for unintentional sin in either community group.
  Hebrews 10:26-29 — Deliberate rejection and threatened judgment: Hebrews likewise distinguishes deliberate sin and rejection of known divine authority from a continuing expectation of sacrifice, while addressing its own covenant setting.

Numbers 15:31
because he treated the word of the Lord as worthless, and scattered His commandments. That soul shall surely be wiped out; his sin is upon him.
  Numbers 15:30 — Same-chapter presumptuous offence explained: Treating the Lord's word as worthless and scattering His commandments explains the preceding presumptuous-hand case and its announced destruction.

Numbers 15:32

  Exodus 31:14-15 — Sabbath work prohibition supplies background: The man is found gathering wood on the Sabbath; Exodus explicitly prohibits Sabbath work and states its death penalty.
  Numbers 15:33-34 — Same-chapter observed act and custody: The observed wood gathering leads to presentation before Moses, Aaron, and the congregation, followed by custody pending determination.

Numbers 15:33

  Leviticus 24:11-12 — Observed offence brought for determination: Both narratives bring the person responsible for an observed act to Moses and then place him in custody awaiting authoritative determination.

Numbers 15:34

  Leviticus 24:12 — Custody awaiting the Lord's instruction: Both cases explicitly place the offender in custody before receiving the Lord's instruction about what should be done.

Numbers 15:35
And the Lord spoke unto Moses, saying: The man must certainly be put to death. Stone him with stones, all the congregation.
  Leviticus 24:14 — Congregational stoning prescribed in distinct cases: The divine command assigns stoning by the congregation in both cases, while the offences remain Sabbath wood gathering and cursing respectively.
  Numbers 15:36 — Same-chapter sentence and execution: The whole congregation carries out the commanded stoning, explicitly described as obedience to the Lord's instruction through Moses.

Numbers 15:36

  Leviticus 24:23 — Outside-camp judgment carried out: Both narratives end with the congregation carrying the offender outside the camp and stoning him as the Lord commanded Moses.

Numbers 15:37
And the Lord said unto Moses, saying:
  Numbers 15:38-41 — Same-chapter speech introduction: This speech introduces garment fringes with blue thread as a reminder to obey commandments and remain holy to Israel's redeemer.

Numbers 15:38

  Deuteronomy 22:12 — Garment tassel instruction: Both instructions require fringes or twisted tassels at garment edges, with Numbers additionally specifying blue thread and the reminder's purpose.
  Matthew 23:5 — Later fringe practice criticized for display: Jesus' criticism concerns enlarged fringes, an identifiable garment feature here prescribed with a blue thread, while His criticism addresses the motive of display.

Numbers 15:39

  Deuteronomy 6:6-9 — Visible reminders and commandment observance: Both instructions connect visible signs with keeping God's commands present in daily life, while assigning different signs and locations.
  Numbers 15:40 — Same-chapter visual reminder and intended obedience: Seeing the fringes is intended to produce the remembering, obedience, and holiness expressly restated in the following verse.

Numbers 15:40
that you may remember and do all My commandments, and be holy unto your God.
  Leviticus 19:2 — Holiness demanded of the congregation: Both instructions require Israel to be holy in relation to the Lord its God, with Numbers connecting that holiness to remembered commandments.

Numbers 15:41
I am the Lord your God, who brought you out of the land of Egypt to be your God. I am the Lord your God.
  Leviticus 11:45 — Exodus grounds belonging and holiness: Both passages identify the Lord as the one who brought Israel from Egypt to be its God and ground holiness in that relationship.

Numbers 16:1
And Korah son of Izhar, son of Kohath, son of Levi, spoke, with Dathan and Abiram, sons of Eliab, and On son of Peleth, son of Reuben.
  Exodus 6:18-21 — Korah's ancestry identified: Exodus names Izhar among Kohath's sons and Korah among Izhar's sons, corresponding to the ancestry stated for the rebel.
  Numbers 26:9 — Dathan and Abiram identified: The later census identifies Dathan and Abiram as Eliab's sons who rose against Moses and Aaron in Korah's congregation.

Numbers 16:2

Numbers 16:3

Numbers 16:4

  Numbers 16:22 — Same-chapter repeated intercessory posture: Moses falls on his face upon hearing the challenge; later Moses and Aaron fall and plead against consuming the congregation.

  2 Timothy 2:19 — Distinctive recognition wording echoed: God's knowing those who are His closely parallels the seal that the Lord knew those who are His, without claiming an exclusively identified quotation source.
  Numbers 17:20-23 — Chosen servant disclosed by a sign: The promise that God identifies His chosen holy servant is followed by the staff sign in which Aaron’s staff for Levi sprouts.

Numbers 16:6

  Numbers 16:17-18 — Same-chapter fire-pan test specified and undertaken: The initial command to take fire pans is detailed for the challengers and then carried out with fire and incense.

Numbers 16:7

  Numbers 17:5 — Incense challenge becomes a warning: The test of who may present incense leads to the later memorial warning that anyone outside Aaron's descendants must not approach with incense.
  Numbers 16:35 — Same-chapter incense test and fatal outcome: Those instructed to place incense before the Lord are the two hundred fifty incense presenters later consumed by fire.

Numbers 16:8

Numbers 16:9

  Numbers 3:6-9 — Levitical privilege already assigned: The service near God and before the congregation corresponds to Levi's earlier appointment to assist Aaron and keep the Tent's charges.

Numbers 16:10

  Numbers 18:6-7 — Levite service distinguished from priesthood: The later law distinguishes the Levites given for Tent ministries from the priesthood expressly assigned to Aaron and his sons.

Numbers 16:11

  Psalms 105:16-18 — Opposition and judgment recalled: The psalm connects hostility against Moses and Aaron with the earth swallowing the rebels and fire burning sinners.

Numbers 16:12

  Numbers 16:25 — Same-chapter refused summons and Moses' approach: Dathan and Abiram refuse Moses' summons, after which Moses himself goes to them accompanied by Israel's elders.

Numbers 16:13

  Exodus 33:3 — Promised-land description turned against the exodus: The rebels apply milk-and-honey language to the land they left, whereas the earlier promise applies it to the destination God will give.
  Numbers 14:2-3 — Recurring accusation of wilderness destruction: Both complaints charge the leaders' exodus with bringing Israel to death in the wilderness and portray Egypt as preferable.

Numbers 16:14

  Numbers 14:8 — Promised-land expectation contested: The rebels challenge Moses over land and inheritance using milk-and-honey language, while Joshua and Caleb had grounded that expected gift in the Lord.

Numbers 16:15

  1 Samuel 12:3-5 — Leader denies exploitation: Moses denies taking desirable property or harming anyone, paralleling Samuel's public denial of seizure, oppression, and personal gain.

Numbers 16:16

  Numbers 16:18-19 — Same-chapter appointed confrontation assembled: The order to be ready before the Lord with Aaron leads to the fire-pan presentation and Korah's gathered congregation at the Tent.

Numbers 16:17
And each of you shall take his fire pan and place incense on it; each shall present his fire pan before the Lord, two hundred fifty fire pans. You and Aaron shall each bring his fire pan.
  Numbers 16:2 — Same-chapter test corresponds to challenger count: The requirement of two hundred fifty fire pans corresponds to the stated number of leading men challenging Moses and Aaron.
  Numbers 16:35 — Same-chapter test participants and consequence: The two hundred fifty incense presenters specified for the test are the men consumed by the Lord's fire.

Numbers 16:18

Numbers 16:19

  Numbers 16:21-22 — Same-chapter speech and immediate intercession: This speech commands separation before consumption, immediately prompting Moses and Aaron to plead about judgment of the whole congregation.

Numbers 16:22

Numbers 16:23

  Numbers 16:24 — Same-chapter speech introduction: This divine speech introduces the particular command for the congregation to withdraw from around Korah's congregation.

Numbers 16:24

  Numbers 16:27 — Same-chapter separation command obeyed: The command to withdraw is followed by the congregation's withdrawal from around Korah's tent before the judgment.

Numbers 16:25

  Numbers 16:12 — Same-chapter refusal and subsequent encounter: Moses' journey to Dathan and Abiram follows their refusal to come when he sent to summon them.

Numbers 16:26

Numbers 16:27

  Numbers 16:24-26 — Same-chapter withdrawal carries out warning: The congregation withdraws as commanded, while Dathan and Abiram stand with their households beside their own tents.

Numbers 16:28

  Numbers 16:30-31 — Same-chapter commission authenticated by announced sign: Moses' claim that the Lord sent him is tested by the predicted opening earth, which splits as he finishes speaking.

Numbers 16:29
If these men die the death of all people, and a visitation comes upon them according to the visitation of all people, then the Lord has not sent me.
  Numbers 16:30 — Same-chapter alternative outcomes define the test: Ordinary death would count against Moses' commission, while the contrasting portent of the opening earth would identify provocation of the Lord.

Numbers 16:30
But if the Lord displays a portent, and the earth opens its mouth and swallows them, their households, their tents, and everything that belongs to them, and they go down alive to Hades, then you shall know that these men have provoked the Lord.
  Deuteronomy 11:6 — Earth-swallowing portent recalled: Deuteronomy recalls the earth opening its mouth upon Dathan, Abiram, their houses, tents, and possessions as a witnessed act of the Lord.
  Numbers 16:31-33 — Same-chapter predicted portent occurs: The specified splitting earth, swallowing, and descent alive to Hades immediately occur in the following narrative.

Numbers 16:31
As he finished speaking all these words, the ground beneath them split apart.
  Numbers 16:30 — Same-chapter announced sign begins immediately: The ground splitting as Moses finishes speaking initiates the extraordinary event he has just specified as the test.

Numbers 16:32
And the earth opened and swallowed them, their households, all the people who were with Korah, and their livestock.
  Deuteronomy 11:6 — Swallowed households and possessions recalled: The later address explicitly remembers the earth swallowing Dathan and Abiram with their houses, tents, and substance.
  Numbers 26:10-11 — Later account preserves survival exception: The census recalls the swallowing and fiery judgment but expressly says Korah's sons did not die, preventing an unqualified claim about every descendant.

Numbers 16:33

  Psalms 105:17 — Earth covers the rebel assembly: The psalm recalls the earth swallowing Dathan and covering Abiram's assembly, corresponding to the engulfing judgment described here.
  Jude 1:11 — Korah's rebellion as later warning: Jude explicitly names perishing in Korah's rebellion as a warning, corresponding to the narrated destruction of this rebellious assembly.

Numbers 16:34
And all Israel around them fled at their cry, saying: Lest the earth swallow us!
  Numbers 16:30-33 — Same-chapter witnessed sign produces fear: Israel's fear of being swallowed responds to the just-completed sign in which the rebels descend and the earth covers them.

Numbers 16:35

  Psalms 105:18 — Fire judgment recalled: The psalm remembers fire kindled in the rebels' assembly and a flame burning sinners, corresponding to the incense presenters' destruction.
  Numbers 17:2-5 — Fire-pan judgment becomes memorial: The next chapter orders recovery of the burned men's bronze fire pans and makes them a memorial against unauthorized incense presentation.

Numbers 17:1
And the Lord said unto Moses
  Numbers 17:2-5 — Same-chapter speech and particular instruction: This speech continues directly into the address to Eleazar and the order to turn the fatal incense trial's bronze fire pans into an altar reminder.

Numbers 19:6

Numbers 19:7

  Leviticus 16:26 — Washing before reentry: The priest washes clothes and bathes before reentering camp, resembling the washing and bathing required of the released-goat handler; Numbers additionally states uncleanness until evening.
  Numbers 19:8 — Same-chapter handlers' purification: Both priest and heifer burner must wash clothes, bathe, and remain unclean until evening, even though their tasks in preparing the rite differ.

Numbers 19:8
And the one who burns it shall wash his clothes and bathe his body, and he shall be unclean until evening.
  Leviticus 16:28 — Burner's purification: Both rites require the person who burns the animal remains to wash garments and bathe; Numbers additionally specifies uncleanness until evening.
  Numbers 19:10 — Same-chapter handlers distinguished: Both the burner and ash gatherer wash garments and are unclean until evening; bathing is expressly required for the burner in this instruction.

Numbers 19:9
And a clean person shall gather the heifer's ashes and deposit them outside the camp in a clean place. They shall be kept for the congregation of the sons of Israel as water of sprinkling; it is a means of purification.
  Hebrews 9:13 — Ashes and sprinkling recalled: The heifer's ashes are reserved for purification water, the same heifer ashes Hebrews explicitly describes as sprinkling the defiled for bodily cleanness.
  Numbers 19:17-18 — Same-chapter reserved ashes put to use: The stored ashes become the material mixed with living water and sprinkled with hyssop on the specified unclean persons and objects.

Numbers 19:10
And the one who gathers the heifer's ashes shall wash his clothes and be unclean until evening. This shall be an everlasting ordinance for the sons of Israel and the attached sojourners.
  Numbers 19:9 — Same-chapter clean collector and resulting obligation: A clean person is appointed to gather the ashes, and the next provision requires that collector to wash garments and remain unclean until evening.
  Numbers 19:21 — Same-chapter purification agents and handlers: The ash gatherer must wash garments and remains unclean until evening; the later rule likewise requires garment washing by the sprinkler and evening uncleanness for water contact.

Numbers 19:11
Whosoever touches a dead person, any human being who has died, shall be unclean for seven days.
  Numbers 6:6-9 — Corpse defilement in consecration: The general rule makes human corpse contact unclean for seven days; the vow law likewise treats a nearby death as defiling and prescribes seventh-day cleansing.
  Leviticus 11:39-40 — Human and animal death impurity contrasted: Human corpse contact brings seven days of uncleanness here, whereas contact with an edible animal's carcass is stated to make a person unclean until evening.

Numbers 19:12
That person shall be purified on the third day and on the seventh day, and he shall be clean. If he is not purified on the third day and on the seventh day, he shall not be clean.
  Numbers 19:19 — Same-chapter schedule and procedure: The third- and seventh-day purification schedule is specified here and then carried out in the later instruction about sprinkling, washing clothes, and bathing.
  Numbers 6:9 — Seventh-day cleansing parallel: The required seventh-day purification corresponds to the vowed person's seventh-day cleansing after sudden nearby death, while this general rule also specifies the third day.

Numbers 19:13
Whosoever touches the dead body of any human being who has died, and is not purified, has defiled the Tent of the Lord; that soul shall be wiped out from Israel. Because water of sprinkling was not sprinkled upon him, he is unclean; his uncleanness is still in him.
  Numbers 5:2-3 — Corpse impurity and the divine dwelling: Unpurified corpse contact defiles the Tent, explaining the earlier command to remove corpse-unclean persons from the camp where the Lord dwells.
  Numbers 19:20 — Same-chapter failure to purify and sanction: Both warnings connect remaining unpurified with defiling the Lord's sanctuary, missing sprinkling water, and destruction from the congregation.

Numbers 19:14
And this is the law: If a person dies in a house, everyone who enters the house and everything in the house shall be unclean for seven days.
  Numbers 6:6 — Entering a death location: The rule that entry into a house containing a dead person brings impurity explains the vowed person's prohibition against entering where any dead person is.
  Numbers 19:18 — Same-chapter house impurity and cleansing: The house, contents, and people made unclean by a death are among the locations, vessels, and persons the clean man is later told to sprinkle.

Numbers 19:15
And every open vessel without a fastening tied upon it is unclean.
  Numbers 19:14 — Same-chapter particular vessel within general case: The open vessel rule specifies an affected item within the preceding case in which a death makes everything inside the house unclean.
  Leviticus 11:33 — Vessel contamination compared: Both laws identify vessels as capable of containing impurity; Numbers specifies an uncovered vessel in a death location, while Leviticus specifies a dead creeping creature falling inside.

Numbers 19:16
And anyone who touches, on the open plain, one slain, a dead person, a human bone, or a tomb shall be unclean for seven days.
  Matthew 23:27 — Tomb imagery and uncleanness: Contact with human bones or a tomb causes impurity here; Jesus' comparison with whitewashed tombs likewise joins dead people's bones and uncleanness as its image.
  Numbers 19:18 — Same-chapter contact cases and cleansing: The slain person, human bone, dead person, and tomb that cause seven-day impurity are specifically repeated among the contacts for which sprinkling is prescribed.

Numbers 19:17
And for the unclean person they shall take some ashes from what was burned for purification, and they shall pour living water over them into a vessel.
  Numbers 19:9 — Same-chapter reserved ashes used: The ashes previously stored for the congregation's purification water are now taken from the burned purification material and mixed with living water.
  Hebrews 9:13 — Heifer ashes and bodily cleansing: Mixing the purification ashes with water prepares the heifer-ash sprinkling that Hebrews explicitly says sanctifies defiled people for bodily cleanness.

Numbers 19:18
And a clean man shall take hyssop, dip it in the water, and sprinkle it around the house, the vessels, and the people who were there, and on whoever touched the human bone, the slain person, the dead person, or the tomb.
  Leviticus 14:51 — Hyssop and house sprinkling: The clean man uses hyssop to sprinkle the house and its contents; the house-purification law likewise uses hyssop among the materials for sprinkling the house.
  Numbers 19:14-16 — Same-chapter impurity cases answered: The prescribed sprinkling addresses the earlier house, vessel, human-remains, and tomb cases rather than treating every form of impurity as identical.

Numbers 19:19
And the clean person shall sprinkle the unclean person on the third day and on the seventh day. He shall be purified on the seventh day, wash his clothes, and bathe in water, and he shall be unclean until evening.
  Leviticus 14:9 — Seventh-day washing and bathing: Both cleansing procedures specify seventh-day garment washing and bodily bathing; Numbers additionally places sprinkling on the third and seventh days and retains evening uncleanness.
  Numbers 19:12 — Same-chapter purification schedule fulfilled: The clean person's third- and seventh-day sprinkling applies the schedule already stated as necessary for the corpse-unclean person's purification.

Numbers 19:20
And if a man is defiled, and is not purified, that soul shall be destroyed from the midst of the congregation, because he has defiled the holy things of the Lord; because water of sprinkling was not sprinkled upon him, he is unclean.
  Numbers 19:13 — Same-chapter sanctuary defilement warning repeated: The second warning repeats that failure to receive purification water leaves uncleanness, defiles the Lord's holy things, and brings removal from the congregation.
  Numbers 5:3 — Camp holiness protected: The sanction for remaining unpurified and defiling holy things corresponds to the earlier demand that unclean persons not defile the camp where the Lord dwells.

Numbers 19:21
And this shall be an everlasting ordinance for you. The one who sprinkles the water of sprinkling shall wash his clothes, and whosoever touches the water of sprinkling shall be unclean until evening.
  Numbers 19:10 — Same-chapter handling obligations compared: The later rule requires the sprinkler to wash clothes and the water toucher to remain unclean until evening, comparable to the ash collector's washing and evening uncleanness.
  Leviticus 15:5 — Contact uncleanness until evening: Touching purification water brings uncleanness until evening here; Leviticus likewise sets evening as the duration after touching a defiled bed, with additional washing and bathing.

Numbers 19:22
And whatever the unclean person touches shall be unclean, and the person who touches it shall be unclean until evening.
  Leviticus 15:7 — Contact impurity compared: Both describe uncleanness passing through contact until evening: Numbers concerns touching what the unclean person affected, while Leviticus concerns touching the discharge sufferer's skin.
  Leviticus 11:32 — Transmission to objects compared: Both laws allow impurity to pass to ordinary articles, but Numbers concerns what the unclean person touches whereas Leviticus concerns an article receiving a dead creature.

Numbers 20:1
And the sons of Israel, the whole congregation, came into the wilderness of Zin in the first month, and the people remained at Kadesh. Miriam died there and was buried there.
  Exodus 15:20-21 — Earlier identification of a named participant: The death notice names Miriam; Exodus identifies Miriam as Aaron's sister and the prophetess who led the women in song after the sea deliverance.

Numbers 20:2
And there was no water for the congregation, and they gathered against Moses and Aaron.
  Exodus 17:1-3 — Repeated wilderness water complaint: Both accounts begin with the congregation lacking drinking water and confronting Moses; the earlier Rephidim complaint also names the threatened livestock.

Numbers 20:3
And the people reviled Moses, saying: If only we had died when our brothers were destroyed before the Lord!
  Numbers 14:2 — Repeated wish for death in rebellion: The wish to have died with the brothers repeats the earlier congregation's wish to have died in Egypt or the wilderness, without identifying which brothers are intended.

Numbers 20:4
And why have you brought the Lord's congregation into this wilderness to kill us and our livestock?
  Exodus 17:3 — Repeated accusation about wilderness thirst: The people accuse their leaders of bringing the congregation and livestock into the wilderness to die; Exodus records the same accusation over thirst.

Numbers 20:5
And why have you brought us up from Egypt to arrive at this wretched place—a place where nothing is sown, with no figs, vines, pomegranates, or water to drink?
  Numbers 13:23-27 — Contrast between wilderness lack and reported land produce: The complaint names missing figs, vines and pomegranates; the scouts had carried grapes, pomegranates and figs and displayed the promised land's fruit.
  Numbers 21:5 — Recurring complaint about departure and provisions: Both speeches blame the departure from Egypt for threatened death in the wilderness and lack of water; the later speech additionally rejects the bread supplied.

Numbers 20:6
And Moses and Aaron went away from the congregation to the entrance of the Tent of Testimony and fell on their faces. The Lord's glory appeared to them.
  Numbers 14:5-10 — Repeated leadership response and divine appearance: Moses and Aaron again fall on their faces during congregational resistance, and the Lord's glory appears in a scene involving the Tent of Testimony.

Numbers 20:7
And the Lord spoke unto Moses, saying:
  Numbers 20:8 — Same-chapter introduction and specified command: The divine speech notice introduces the particular instruction to take the staff, gather the congregation, address the rock and provide drinking water.

Numbers 20:8
Take the staff, and gather the congregation, you and Aaron your brother; and speak unto the rock before them, and it shall give its waters; and you shall bring water out of the rock for them, and shall give the congregation and their livestock to drink.
  Exodus 17:5-6 — Parallel provision with contrasting rock instructions: Both commands involve Moses, a staff and water from a rock; Exodus commands striking at Horeb, whereas this command tells the brothers to speak to the rock.
  Numbers 20:11 — Same-chapter command and narrated action: The promised water is for congregation and livestock; the subsequent action supplies both, while describing two strokes rather than the commanded speech.

Numbers 20:9
And Moses took the staff that was before the Lord, as the Lord had commanded.
  Numbers 20:8 — Same-chapter command and initial compliance: Moses takes the staff as instructed in the preceding command; the statement does not identify whose staff it is beyond its position before the Lord.

Numbers 20:10
And Moses and Aaron assembled the congregation before the rock. He said to them: Listen to me, you disobedient people! Surely we are not to bring water out of this rock for you?
  Psalms 105:32-33 — Later poetic remembrance of Moses at the water: Numbers preserves Moses' rebuke and question at the rock; the psalm recalls his harm at the water of contradiction and a command issued with his lips.
  Numbers 20:12 — Same-chapter speech and divine assessment: Moses challenges the disobedient people about bringing water from the rock; the following divine assessment concerns the brothers' unbelief and failure to show God holy.

Numbers 20:11
And Moses raised his hand and struck the rock twice with the staff. Abundant water came out, and the congregation and their livestock drank.
  Exodus 17:6 — Parallel rock-water events with distinct instructions: Water follows striking in both accounts, but Horeb's command explicitly requires striking; here Moses strikes twice after a command to speak.
  1 Corinthians 10:4 — Later interpretation of wilderness rock-water provision: Numbers describes the congregation drinking water from a rock; Paul recalls wilderness spiritual drink and identifies the spiritual rock as Christ, without uniquely naming this episode.
  Psalms 135:16 — Poetic commemoration of wilderness rock-water provision: The psalm praises the Lord for bringing water from a sheer rock; Numbers narrates abundant rock water sufficient for the congregation and livestock.

Numbers 20:12
And the Lord said unto Moses and Aaron: Because you did not believe so as to show Me holy before the sons of Israel, you shall not bring this congregation into the land that I have given them.
  Numbers 27:12-14 — Explicit later explanation of exclusion from leading entry: The later mountain command recalls the failure to sanctify God at Kadesh's Water of Dispute and relates Moses' approaching death to Aaron's exclusion.
  Deuteronomy 32:50-52 — Later account of the same leadership judgment: Deuteronomy likewise explains the brothers' deaths before entry through their disobedience and failure to sanctify God at the water of dispute in Zin.

Numbers 20:13
This is the Water of Dispute, because the sons of Israel reviled before the Lord, and He was shown holy among them.
  Numbers 27:14 — Explicit identification of the Water of Dispute: The later passage names the Water of Dispute at Kadesh in Zin and recalls the congregation's resistance and failure to sanctify God there.
  Psalms 105:32-33 — Poetic recollection of the water controversy: Numbers names the water from Israel's dispute and God's being shown holy; the psalm recalls anger at the water of contradiction and harm to Moses.

Numbers 20:14
And Moses sent messengers from Kadesh to the king of Edom, saying: Thus says your brother Israel: You know all the hardship that has overtaken us.
  Genesis 36:6-8 — Kinship background for the address to Edom: Israel addresses Edom as brother; Genesis identifies Esau as Edom and describes his separation from Jacob his brother to live in Seir.
  Judges 11:17 — Later diplomatic recollection of the Edom request: Jephthah's account specifically remembers Israel sending the king of Edom a request to pass through, matching Moses' embassy from Kadesh.

Numbers 20:15
And our fathers went down to Egypt, and we lived as sojourners in Egypt many days. The Egyptians mistreated us and our fathers.
  Genesis 46:5-7 — Earlier narrative behind the descent-to-Egypt recital: The embassy recalls the fathers going down to Egypt; Genesis narrates Jacob and his entire family entering Egypt with their possessions.
  Exodus 3:7-9 — Earlier testimony to Egyptian oppression: Moses' recital describes Egyptian mistreatment of Israel and its fathers; the earlier divine speech acknowledges Israel's affliction, taskmasters, cry and oppression.

Numbers 20:16
And we cried out unto the Lord, and the Lord heard our voice; He sent an angel and brought us out of Egypt. Now we are at Kadesh, a town at the edge of your territory.
  Exodus 3:7-10 — Earlier account of the heard cry and deliverance: Both passages connect Israel's cry under Egyptian oppression with the Lord hearing and rescuing them; this comparison does not assign an identity to the reported angel.

Numbers 20:17
We will pass through your land. We will not cross fields or vineyards or drink water from your cistern. We will travel the royal road and turn neither right nor left until we have passed through your territory.
  Numbers 21:22 — Parallel terms of requests for peaceful transit: Both requests promise use of the royal road without entering fields or vineyards or taking the landholder's water; Edom and Sihon remain distinct recipients.

Numbers 20:18
And Edom said to him: You shall not pass through me; otherwise I will come out to meet you in war.
  Judges 11:17 — Later recollection of Edom's refusal: The king forbids transit and threatens war; Jephthah's later historical recital remembers that Edom's king would not listen to Israel's passage request.

Numbers 20:19
And the sons of Israel say unto him: We will pass alongside the mountain. If I and the livestock drink your water, I will pay you its price. The matter is nothing; we will pass alongside the mountain.
  Deuteronomy 2:4-6 — Related water-purchase provision in dealings with Esau's sons: Israel offers payment if people or livestock drink Edom's water; Deuteronomy commands buying food and measured water from the sons of Esau.
  Numbers 20:17 — Same-chapter renewal with an added payment offer: The renewed request adds payment for any water consumed after the earlier promise not to drink from Edom's cistern, preserving the negotiation's distinct terms.

Numbers 20:20
He said: You shall not pass through me. Edom came out to meet him with a large force and a strong hand.
  Numbers 20:18 — Same-chapter threat followed by military mobilization: Edom had threatened to come out for war if Israel passed through; its appearance with a large force and strong hand gives that threat military form.

Numbers 20:21
And Edom was unwilling to allow Israel to pass through his territory, so Israel turned away from him.
  Judges 11:17-18 — Later recollection of refusal and the detour: Numbers reports refusal followed by Israel turning away; Jephthah recalls the rejected request and Israel's subsequent route around Edom and Moab.
  Deuteronomy 2:4-8 — Related policy and route around Esau's territory: Israel turns away instead of forcing entry; Deuteronomy forbids battle for Esau's allotted land and describes passing by the brothers in Seir.

Numbers 20:22
And they set out from Kadesh, and the sons of Israel, the whole congregation, arrived at Mount Hor.
  Numbers 33:37 — Parallel itinerary entry: Both passages explicitly record departure from Kadesh and arrival at Mount Hor; the itinerary additionally locates the mountain near Edom.

Numbers 20:23
And the Lord said unto Moses and Aaron at Mount Hor, on the border of the land of Edom:
  Numbers 33:37-38 — Later itinerary locating the mountain command: The speech is located at Mount Hor on Edom's border; the itinerary places Hor near Edom and says Aaron ascended by the Lord's command.

Numbers 20:24
Let Aaron be gathered to his people, for you shall certainly not enter the land that I have given to the sons of Israel, because you provoked Me at the Water of Reviling.
  Deuteronomy 32:50-51 — Later recollection of Aaron's death and its stated cause: Deuteronomy recalls Aaron dying at Mount Hor and links the brothers' fate to disobedience at Kadesh's water, matching the exclusion explained here.
  Numbers 20:12 — Same-chapter application of the exclusion judgment: The earlier speech bars both brothers from bringing the congregation into the land; this command applies that judgment to Aaron while recalling the Water of Reviling.

Numbers 20:25
Take Aaron and his son Eleazar and bring them up Mount Hor before the whole congregation.
  Numbers 20:27 — Same-chapter ascent command and compliance: The instruction to take Aaron and Eleazar up Mount Hor before the congregation is followed by Moses carrying out the ascent publicly.

Numbers 20:26
And strip Aaron of his robe and clothe his son Eleazar. Let Aaron be gathered and die there.
  Exodus 29:29-30 — Priestly garment succession and its enactment: Aaron's robe is assigned to Eleazar here; Exodus had stipulated that Aaron's holy garments belong to the son succeeding him in priestly ministry.
  Numbers 20:28 — Same-chapter succession command and execution: The order to strip Aaron, clothe Eleazar and let Aaron die on the mountain is followed by the transfer of garments and Aaron's death.

Numbers 20:27
And Moses did as the Lord commanded, and he brought him up Mount Hor before the whole congregation.
  Numbers 20:25-26 — Same-chapter compliance with the public ascent order: Moses brings Aaron up before the whole congregation, matching the ordered public ascent and preparing for the garment transfer described in the command.

Numbers 20:28
And he stripped Aaron of his garments and put them on his son Eleazar. Aaron died on the summit of the mountain, and Moses and Eleazar came down from the mountain.
  Exodus 29:29-30 — Enactment of hereditary priestly garment succession: Moses puts Aaron's garments on Eleazar at Aaron's death; Exodus provides for the successor son to wear Aaron's consecrated garments.
  Deuteronomy 10:6 — Parallel death and priestly succession notice with distinct locations: Both notices associate Aaron's death with Eleazar succeeding him; Numbers names Mount Hor, while Deuteronomy names Misadai, without an asserted equation of those places.
  Numbers 33:38-39 — Later itinerary's notice of Aaron's death: The itinerary also records Aaron dying on Hor, adding his age and the fortieth-year date that this mountain narrative does not itself specify.

Numbers 20:29
And the whole congregation saw that Aaron had been released, and all the house of Israel wept for Aaron thirty days.
  Deuteronomy 34:8 — Parallel thirty-day communal mourning for leaders: All Israel mourns Aaron for thirty days; the later Moses death account likewise records thirty days of weeping by Israel in Moab.

Numbers 21:1
And the Canaanite king of Arad, who lived by the wilderness, heard—for Israel had come by the road of Atharim—and fought Israel, and they took captives from them.
  Numbers 33:40 — Later itinerary's notice of the king of Arad: Both passages name the Canaanite king of Arad hearing of Israel's approach; this narrative adds the attack and the taking of captives.

Numbers 21:2
And Israel vowed a vow unto the Lord: If Thou hand this people over into my power, I will devote them and their towns to destruction.
  Numbers 21:3 — Same-chapter conditional vow and narrated fulfilment: Israel promises to devote the people and towns if the Lord hands them over; the following verse reports both the victory and the promised devotion.

Numbers 21:3
And the Lord heard Israel's voice and handed the Canaanite over into his power. Israel devoted him and his towns to destruction, and they called that place Devotion to Destruction.
  Numbers 21:2 — Same-chapter victory and fulfilment of the vow: The Lord's hearing, the enemy's delivery and the towns' devotion answer the preceding conditional vow, which explains the resulting place-name in this account.

Numbers 21:4
And they set out from Mount Hor by the road toward the Red Sea and went around the land of Edom. The people became fainthearted on the journey.
  Deuteronomy 2:1-8 — Parallel description of the detour around Edom: Numbers describes leaving Hor toward the Red Sea and going around Edom; Deuteronomy recalls the Red Sea route, circling Seir and passing the sons of Esau.
  Numbers 20:21 — Narrative consequence of the refused passage: The detour around Edom follows the earlier refusal of transit and Israel's decision to turn away rather than pass through Edom's territory.

Numbers 21:5
And the people spoke against God and against Moses: Why have you brought us out of Egypt to kill us in the wilderness? There is no bread or water, and our soul is disgusted with this empty bread.
  Numbers 20:4-5 — Recurring accusation about Egypt, wilderness death and water: The later complaint again blames the exodus for threatened wilderness death and missing water; its rejection of the bread differs from the earlier list of absent produce.
  Deuteronomy 8:14-16 — Later recollection of the wilderness hazards and provision: Deuteronomy joins deliverance from Egypt, thirst, biting serpents and manna in its warning against forgetting God; Numbers preserves the complaint amid those wilderness provisions.

Numbers 21:6
And the Lord sent deadly serpents among the people. They bit the people, and many of the sons of Israel died.
  1 Corinthians 10:9 — Explicit later warning from the serpent judgment: Paul warns against testing Christ as people did who were destroyed by serpents; Numbers narrates deadly serpents and many Israelite deaths following the complaint.
  Deuteronomy 8:15 — Later recollection of biting serpents in the wilderness: Numbers describes the deadly serpent attacks; Deuteronomy recalls biting serpents among the dangers of the great and fearful wilderness through which God led Israel.

Numbers 21:7
And the people came unto Moses and said: We have sinned by speaking against the Lord and against you. Therefore pray unto the Lord, and let Him remove the serpents from us. Moses prayed unto the Lord for the people.
  Numbers 11:2 — Parallel appeal to Moses and intercessory prayer: The people ask Moses to pray over the serpents and he does; the earlier fire crisis likewise brings their appeal and Moses' prayer for relief.
  Numbers 21:5 — Same-chapter confession naming the preceding offense: The people confess speaking against the Lord and Moses, identifying the same two recipients of the complaint about Egypt, water and bread.

Numbers 21:8
And the Lord said unto Moses: Make a serpent for yourself, and set it upon a standard; and it shall be, if a serpent bites a man, that everyone bitten, when he looks upon it, shall live.
  John 3:14-15 — Explicit later comparison with the lifted wilderness serpent: The appointed serpent is raised so bitten people can look and live; Jesus explicitly compares Moses lifting the serpent with the Son of Man's lifting and eternal life.
  Numbers 21:9 — Same-chapter healing instruction and implementation: The command promises life to a bitten person who looks at the raised serpent; Moses makes the bronze serpent and the narrative reports that response.

Numbers 21:9
And Moses made a bronze serpent and set it on a standard. Whenever a serpent bit a person, that person looked at the bronze serpent and lived.
  John 3:14-15 — Explicit later use of Moses' raised serpent: John names Moses' lifting of the wilderness serpent as the comparison for the Son of Man's lifting; Numbers records the bronze object's erection and the bitten people's survival.
  2 Kings 18:4 — Later history of the bronze serpent Moses made: The later reform account identifies Moses' bronze serpent and its destruction after Israelites burned incense to it, extending the object's history beyond this healing episode.

Numbers 21:10
And the sons of Israel set out and camped at Oboth.
  Numbers 33:43-44 — Parallel itinerary centred on Oboth: The shorter narrative records the camp at Oboth; the longer itinerary likewise names Oboth as a camp and the next departure point.

Numbers 21:11
And they set out from Oboth and camped at Achel-gai, on the farther side, in the wilderness opposite Moab toward the sunrise.
  Numbers 33:44 — Parallel itinerary with differing destination names: Both record setting out from Oboth for a camp by Moab; this account names Achel-gai and the itinerary Gai, preserving the different destination forms.

Numbers 21:12
From there they set out and camped in the ravine of Zered.
  Deuteronomy 2:13-14 — Parallel notice of the Zered stage: Numbers names the ravine of Zered as the next camp; Deuteronomy recalls crossing that ravine and supplies the elapsed time from Kadesh-barnea.

Numbers 21:13
And from there they set out and camped beyond the Arnon, in the wilderness extending from the Amorite borders. The Arnon is the border of Moab, between Moab and the Amorites.
  Judges 11:18 — Explicit later recollection of the Arnon frontier: Both describe camping beyond the Arnon outside Moab and explicitly identify the Arnon as Moab's boundary; Judges places this within the detour narrative.
  Deuteronomy 2:24 — Related frontier crossing before the Sihon campaign: Numbers locates Israel beyond the Arnon at the Amorite border; Deuteronomy orders crossing the Arnon ravine and beginning the encounter with Sihon's territory.

Numbers 21:14
Therefore it is said in a book: A war of the Lord set Zoob ablaze, and the torrents of the Arnon,
  Numbers 21:13 — Same-chapter geographical connection of quotation and narrative: The cited book's war line mentions the torrents of the Arnon; the preceding narrative locates Israel there and explains the river's frontier role.

Numbers 21:15
and he appointed the torrents for the settlement of Er, and it lies beside the borders of Moab.
  Numbers 21:13 — Same-chapter quoted geography and frontier explanation: The quotation places torrents and Er beside Moab's borders; the narrative immediately before it identifies the Arnon as the boundary between Moab and the Amorites.

Numbers 21:16
And from there they went to the well. This is the well of which the Lord said unto Moses: Gather the people, and I will give them water to drink.
  Numbers 21:17-18 — Same-chapter promised water and the well song: The well is introduced through God's promise to gather the people and give water; the following song celebrates that well and the rulers' digging.

Numbers 21:17
Then Israel sang this song over the well: Lead the singing for it!
  Numbers 21:16 — Same-chapter song and its identified occasion: Israel's song is explicitly over the well; the preceding notice identifies that well with the Lord's instruction to gather the people and provide water.

Numbers 21:18
A well: rulers dug it; kings of nations hewed it out in their kingdom, when they exercised dominion. From the well they went to Manthanain.
  Numbers 21:16-17 — Same-chapter song's account of the promised well: The song describes rulers digging and kings hewing the well, expanding the well provision and singing introduced in the preceding two verses.

Numbers 21:19
And from Manthanain they went to Nahaliel, and from Nahaliel to Bamoth.
  Numbers 21:18 — Same-chapter connected itinerary stages: The preceding verse ends the journey from the well at Manthanain; this verse takes Manthanain as its departure point and continues through Nahaliel to Bamoth.

Numbers 21:20
And from Bamoth they went to a valley in the plain of Moab, from the summit of the Hewn Height overlooking the wilderness.
  Numbers 21:19 — Same-chapter connected itinerary stages: The previous stage ends at Bamoth; this verse resumes there and describes a valley in Moab's plain and a summit overlooking the wilderness.

Numbers 21:21
And Moses sent envoys to Sihon king of the Amorites with words of peace, saying:
  Deuteronomy 2:26 — Parallel account of the embassy to Sihon: Both record Moses sending peace envoys to Sihon; Numbers calls him king of the Amorites, while Deuteronomy names Heshbon and the departure from Kedemoth.
  Judges 11:19 — Later historical recollection of the Sihon embassy: Jephthah specifically recalls Israel sending messengers to Sihon king of Heshbon with a request to pass through, matching this diplomatic opening.

Numbers 21:22
We will pass through your land; we will travel by the road. We will not turn aside into fields or vineyards or drink water from your well. We will travel the royal road until we have passed through your territory.
  Numbers 20:17 — Parallel terms of diplomatic passage requests: The request to Sihon repeats the royal-road pledge and protection of fields, vineyards and water in the earlier embassy to Edom.
  Deuteronomy 2:27-28 — Parallel passage request with differing provisions for water: Both ask peaceful road transit; Numbers promises not to drink the well's water, whereas Deuteronomy's wording requests food and water for payment.

Numbers 21:23
And Sihon would not allow Israel to pass through his territory. He gathered all his people and went out into the wilderness to array himself against Israel. He came to Jahaz and arrayed himself against Israel.
  Deuteronomy 2:30-32 — Parallel refusal and battle assembly at Jahaz: Both recount Sihon's refusal of transit and his coming with all his people to battle at Jahaz; Deuteronomy also states its theological explanation of the refusal.
  Judges 11:20 — Later recollection of refusal and the battle location: Jephthah's recital names Sihon's refusal, gathering of his people and fighting at Jahaz, matching the narrative's specific sequence and location.

Numbers 21:24
And Israel struck him by slaughter with the sword, and they took control of his land from the Arnon to the Jabbok, as far as the sons of Ammon, because Jazer is the border of the sons of Ammon.
  Judges 11:21-22 — Later recollection of victory and territorial limits: Judges likewise records Sihon's defeat and Israel's inheritance of Amorite territory from the Arnon to the Jabbok, the limits explicitly stated here.
  Deuteronomy 2:36-37 — Parallel extent of conquest and exclusion of Ammon: Numbers places the conquest between Arnon and Jabbok up to Ammon's boundary; Deuteronomy describes captured towns while excluding the Ammonite land and Jabbok border.

Numbers 21:25
And Israel took all these towns and settled in all the Amorite towns, in Heshbon and all the towns adjoining it.
  Judges 11:26 — Later recollection of occupation of Heshbon and its towns: Numbers reports Israel settling Heshbon and adjoining Amorite towns; Jephthah later points to Israel's occupation of Heshbon and its dependent towns.
  Deuteronomy 2:34-36 — Parallel capture of Sihon's towns: Both narratives describe taking Sihon's towns after his defeat; Deuteronomy expands the account with their destruction, spoil and extent toward Gilead.

Numbers 21:26
Heshbon was the city of Sihon king of the Amorites, who had previously fought the king of Moab, and they took all his land from Aroer as far as the Arnon.
  Numbers 21:27-29 — Same-chapter historical explanation and the Heshbon poem: The prose identifies Heshbon as Sihon's city and recalls his earlier conquest of Moab; the following poem names his city and Moabite captives.

Numbers 21:27
Therefore those who speak in riddles will say: Come to Heshbon, so that Sihon's city may be built and prepared.
  Numbers 21:26 — Same-chapter poetic city-name and preceding historical identification: The invitation to build and prepare Sihon's city refers to Heshbon, which the preceding prose identifies as his city after an earlier Moabite war.

Numbers 21:28
For fire went out from Heshbon, a flame from Sihon's city. It consumed as far as Moab and swallowed the pillars of the Arnon.
  Numbers 21:26 — Same-chapter poetic conquest imagery and prose setting: The fire from Heshbon consuming toward Moab belongs with the preceding statement that Sihon had fought Moab and taken its land along the Arnon.

Numbers 21:29
Woe unto you, Moab! You are ruined, people of Chemosh. Their sons were handed over to escape, and their daughters as captives to Sihon king of the Amorites.
  Numbers 21:26 — Same-chapter poetic captives and the preceding conquest notice: The poem describes Moab's sons escaping and daughters becoming Sihon's captives; the preceding prose supplies the setting of Sihon's earlier war against Moab.

Numbers 21:30
And their seed shall perish, from Heshbon as far as Dibon; and the women kindled still more fire against Moab.
  Numbers 21:28-29 — Same-chapter continuation of the Heshbon war poem: The line describes seed perishing from Heshbon to Dibon and women burning against Moab, continuing the preceding poem's fire, ruin and captivity.

Numbers 21:31
Israel settled in all the towns of the Amorites.
  Judges 11:21-22 — Later recollection of Amorite occupation: The summary that Israel settled the Amorite towns matches Jephthah's recital of inheriting the Amorites' land after the Lord delivered Sihon.
  Numbers 21:25 — Same-chapter summary and specified occupied towns: This settlement summary returns to the earlier statement that Israel occupied Heshbon and the adjoining Amorite towns, identifying the previously described settlement.

Numbers 21:32
And Moses sent men to inspect Jazer. They captured it and its villages and drove out the Amorites who lived there.
  Judges 11:26 — Later recollection of occupation of Jazer and its villages: Moses' reconnaissance leads to taking Jazer and its villages; Jephthah's later territorial argument explicitly names Israel's occupation of Jazer and its dependent towns.

Numbers 21:33
And they turned and went up by the road to Bashan. Og king of Bashan came out to meet them with all his people, for battle at Edrei.
  Deuteronomy 3:1 — Parallel account of Og's encounter at Edrei: Both accounts describe turning toward Bashan and Og coming with all his people to fight at Edrei, preserving king, region and battle location.
  Joshua 12:4-6 — Later summary of Og's kingdom and defeat: Joshua names Og king of Bashan in Edrei and includes him among the kings defeated by Moses and Israel, matching this battle's named opponent.

Numbers 21:34
And the Lord said unto Moses: Do not fear him, for I have handed him, all his people, and all his land into your hands. You shall do to him as you did to Sihon king of the Amorites, who lived in Heshbon.
  Deuteronomy 3:2 — Parallel assurance and comparison with Sihon's defeat: Both divine speeches forbid fear, promise Og and his people and land to Moses, and explicitly compare the outcome with Sihon of Heshbon.
  Numbers 21:24 — Same-chapter promised comparison and the prior Sihon victory: The command promises to treat Og as Sihon was treated; the preceding campaign records Israel striking Sihon and taking his land.

Numbers 21:35
And he struck him, and his sons, and all his people, until he left none of his alive; and they inherited their land.
  Deuteronomy 3:3-7 — Parallel defeat and inheritance of Og's land: Deuteronomy likewise records Og and his people struck without surviving seed, followed by capture of his cities and plunder of his territory.
  Psalms 134:10-12 — Later poetic remembrance of Og's defeat and inheritance: The psalm names Og of Bashan among the kings struck and their land given to Israel as inheritance, matching the victory and possession reported here.
  Psalms 135:20-22 — Later thanksgiving for Og's defeat and inherited land: The thanksgiving explicitly names Og king of Bashan and the gift of the kings' land to Israel, corresponding to this defeat and inherited territory.

Numbers 22:1
And the sons of Israel set out and camped on the western side of Moab, beside the Jordan opposite Jericho.
  Numbers 33:48 — Matching Jordan-side camp opposite Jericho: The itinerary likewise places Israel on the western side of Moab beside the Jordan opposite Jericho, repeating all three geographical features of this camp notice.

Numbers 22:2
And Balak son of Zippor saw all that Israel had done to the Amorites.
  Numbers 21:23-25 — Earlier Amorite victory seen by Balak: The preceding campaign records Sihon’s refusal, battle and Israel’s capture of Amorite towns; this notice says Balak saw what Israel had done to the Amorites.
  Joshua 24:8-9 — Later recollection joins Amorite conquest and Balak’s response: Joshua’s recital places the Amorite defeat and possession immediately before Balak’s action against Israel, corresponding to the victory that Balak sees before initiating his plan here.

Numbers 22:3
And Moab was very afraid of the people because they were numerous; Moab was distressed by the sons of Israel.
  Numbers 22:6 — Same-chapter fear and the stated rationale for a curse: Moab fears Israel’s numerous people; Balak’s request later expressly says Israel is stronger than Moab and asks for a curse to enable an attack and expulsion.

Numbers 22:4
And Moab said to the council of elders of Midian: Now this congregation will lick up everyone around us as a calf licks up the green plants of the plain. Balak son of Zippor was king of Moab at that time.
  Numbers 22:7 — Same-chapter Midianite council involvement and the joint embassy: Moab addresses Midian’s elders here; the later embassy explicitly includes both Moabite and Midianite councils carrying Balak’s words to Balaam.

Numbers 22:5
And he sent envoys to Balaam son of Beor at Pethor, beside the river in the land of his people, to summon him: Behold, a people has come out of Egypt. They cover the face of the land and are encamped next to me.
  Deuteronomy 23:5 — Later law expressly recalls Balaam’s engagement against Israel: Both name Balaam son of Beor in the approach against Israel; Deuteronomy describes hiring him from Mesopotamia to curse, while this opening locates his summons at riverside Pethor.
  Joshua 24:9 — Later recollection names Balak’s summons to Balaam: Joshua’s recital explicitly names Balak son of Zippor, king of Moab, sending for Balaam to curse Israel, the commission begun by these envoys and continued in the next source verse.

Numbers 22:6
And now come and curse this people for me, for they are stronger than we are. If we can strike some of them, I will drive them from the land. I know that those you bless are blessed, and those you curse are cursed.
  Numbers 23:8 — Requested curse confronted by Balaam’s oracle: Balak asks Balaam to curse Israel for a military advantage; the oracle instead asks how he can curse those whom the Lord does not curse.
  Micah 6:5 — Later prophetic command recalls Balak’s plan and Balaam’s answer: Micah expressly tells Israel to remember Balak king of Moab’s plan and Balaam son of Beor’s answer; this demand for a curse supplies the particular plan being answered.

Numbers 22:7
And the council of elders of Moab and the council of elders of Midian went with the means of divination in their hands. They came to Balaam and told him Balak's words.
  Numbers 22:4 — Same-chapter joint envoys follow the Midianite consultation: The earlier scene has Moab addressing Midian’s elders; this report identifies the resulting Moabite and Midianite councils going together with divination means and Balak’s message.

Numbers 22:8
And he said to them: Lodge here for the night, and I will answer you with whatever the Lord speaks to me. The officials of Moab remained with Balaam.
  Numbers 22:9-12 — Same-chapter overnight consultation and the divine answer: Balaam promises an answer after hearing the Lord and asks the officials to stay overnight; God then comes, questions him, and forbids both going and cursing the blessed people.

Numbers 22:9
And God came to Balaam and said to him: Who are these men with you?
  Numbers 22:10-12 — Same-chapter divine question answered with the commission: God asks who the visiting men are; Balaam identifies Balak’s embassy and repeats its demand before receiving God’s specific prohibition on going and cursing.

Numbers 22:10
And Balaam said to God: Balak son of Zippor, king of Moab, has sent them to me, saying:
  Numbers 22:4-6 — Same-chapter identification of the original sender and purpose: Balaam identifies Balak son of Zippor as the sender; the earlier embassy narrative names that king and records the summons to curse and drive Israel from the land.

Numbers 22:11
Behold, a people has come out of Egypt and covers the face of the land; they are encamped next to me. Now come and curse them for me. Perhaps I can strike them and drive them from the land.
  Numbers 22:5-6 — Same-chapter embassy message repeated before God: The report repeats the people from Egypt covering the land and the request to curse them so they can be struck and expelled, matching the original embassy’s stated purpose.

Numbers 22:12
And God said to Balaam: You shall not go with them or curse the people, for they are blessed.
  Deuteronomy 23:5-6 — Later recollection of the curse’s reversal into blessing: The law explicitly recalls hiring Balaam and God’s turning the intended curse into blessing; this initial answer forbids cursing Israel because the people are already blessed.
  Numbers 22:13 — Same-chapter prohibition and Balaam’s reported refusal: God tells Balaam not to go with the envoys; the following morning Balaam sends them back, explaining that God does not permit his going.

Numbers 22:13
And Balaam rose in the morning and said to Balak's officials: Hurry back to your lord, for God does not permit me to go with you.
