  Numbers 22:12 — Same-chapter Balaam communicates the travel prohibition: Balaam’s morning explanation that God does not permit him to accompany the officials follows the preceding express command not to go with them or curse Israel.

Numbers 22:14
And the officials of Moab rose, came to Balak, and said: Balaam is unwilling to come with us.
  Numbers 22:13 — Same-chapter reported refusal with different supplied explanations: The officials tell Balak that Balaam is unwilling to come; the preceding words attribute Balaam’s refusal to God’s permission, preserving the differing reports without assigning deception as their motive.

Numbers 22:15
And Balak again sent officials, more numerous and more honoured than these.
  Numbers 22:16-17 — Same-chapter second embassy and its intensified invitation: The newly sent officials are more numerous and honoured; their message then urges Balaam to come and promises rich honour and compliance in return for cursing Israel.

Numbers 22:16
And they came to Balaam and say unto him: Thus says Balak son of Zippor: I ask you, do not hesitate to come to me.
  Numbers 22:15 — Same-chapter second officials deliver their sender’s renewed request: These officials deliver Balak son of Zippor’s renewed invitation; the preceding notice identifies them as the larger, more honoured group he sent after the first embassy returned.

Numbers 22:17
I will honour you richly, and I will do for you whatever you say. Come and curse this people for me.
  Numbers 22:18 — Same-chapter offered honour and the stated limit on acceptance: Balak promises abundant honour and compliance if Balaam curses Israel; Balaam answers that even a house full of silver and gold cannot authorize his transgressing God’s word.
  Jude 1:11 — Later Balaam-for-pay reception distinguished from this offered reward: Jude explicitly describes Balaam’s error for pay; this message records Balak’s offer of rich honour, without itself stating Balaam’s inward motive or being identified as Jude’s uniquely quoted verse.

Numbers 22:18
And Balaam answered Balak's officials: Even if Balak gave me his house full of silver and gold, I could not transgress the word of the Lord God to do anything small or great according to my own intention.
  Numbers 24:12-13 — Balaam later repeats the houseful-of-silver-and-gold declaration: Balaam later explicitly recalls telling the messengers that Balak’s silver and gold cannot authorize violating the Lord’s word, repeating the particular reward example and speech constraint stated here.

Numbers 22:19
And now you also remain here tonight, and I will learn what more the Lord will say to me.
  Numbers 22:20 — Same-chapter further overnight inquiry and its answer: Balaam asks the second embassy to remain overnight while he learns more; God then comes at night with a conditional instruction to follow and a requirement to do His word.

Numbers 22:20
And God came to Balaam at night and said to him: If these men are here to summon you, rise and follow them. But you shall do what I tell you.
  Numbers 22:35 — Same-chapter permission to accompany with an express word constraint: The night instruction conditionally permits following the callers but requires obedience; the angel later permits going with the men while specifically constraining Balaam’s speech to the given word.

Numbers 22:21
And Balaam rose in the morning, saddled his donkey, and went with the officials of Moab.
  Numbers 22:20 — Same-chapter departure after the conditional night instruction: The preceding instruction allows Balaam to rise and follow if the men have come to summon him; this report narrates his morning preparation and departure, without explaining the next verse’s anger beyond its wording.

Numbers 22:22
And God grew angry because he went. God's angel rose to obstruct him. Balaam was riding his donkey, with his two servants beside him.
  Numbers 22:32 — Same-chapter angelic opposition and the stated evaluation of the way: The angel later expressly says he opposes Balaam because his way is not fitting; this elaborates the obstruction amid the reported divine anger without supplying a financial motive or reconstructing why permission and anger coexist.

Numbers 22:23
And the donkey saw God's angel opposing them in the road with his sword drawn in his hand. She turned off the road into the plain, and he struck her with the staff to direct her onto the road.
  Numbers 22:31 — Same-chapter donkey’s early sight and Balaam’s later opened eyes: The donkey sees the drawn-sword angel and leaves the road; Balaam sees that opposing angel only after God uncovers his eyes, preserving the narrative’s contrast in perception.

Numbers 22:24
And God's angel stood in the furrows of the vineyards, with a fence on either side.
  Numbers 22:25 — Same-chapter confined vineyard setting and the crushed foot: The angel stands between fences in the vineyard furrows; the following encounter places the donkey against a wall and crushes Balaam’s foot, giving a particular consequence within that confined setting.

Numbers 22:25
And the donkey saw God's angel and pressed herself against the wall, crushing Balaam's foot. He whipped her again.
  Numbers 22:23 — Same-chapter repeated beating with different avoidance actions: The first encounter has the donkey turn into the plain and receive a blow; this encounter has her press against a wall, injure Balaam’s foot, and be whipped again.

Numbers 22:26
And God's angel went farther and stood in a narrow place where there was no way to turn right or left.
  Numbers 22:27 — Same-chapter blocked turning space and the donkey’s next response: The angel occupies a narrow place with no right-or-left turn; the next report records the donkey seeing him and crouching beneath Balaam, rather than adding an unstated escape route.

Numbers 22:27
And the donkey saw God's angel and crouched beneath Balaam. Balaam became angry and beat the donkey with his staff.
  Numbers 22:28 — Same-chapter beating and the donkey’s three-blow complaint: Balaam angrily beats the crouching donkey; God then opens her mouth and she expressly asks why he has struck her this third time, identifying the occasion of her complaint.

Numbers 22:28
And God opened the donkey's mouth, and she says to Balaam: What have I done unto you that you have struck me this third time?
  2 Peter 2:15-16 — Later reception of the speaking beast’s rebuke: The later warning describes a speechless beast speaking with a human voice and restraining the prophet; this account narrates God opening the donkey’s mouth, preserving Numbers’ Beor and the target’s Bosor wording without harmonizing patronymics.
  Numbers 22:23-27 — Same-chapter third-blow complaint and its preceding encounters: The donkey’s third-time complaint follows the narrated encounters in the plain, by the wall, and in the narrow place, with Balaam striking or whipping her in each scene.

Numbers 22:29
And Balaam said to the donkey: Because you have mocked me! If I had a sword in my hand, I would have stabbed you already.
  Numbers 22:31-33 — Same-chapter wished-for sword and the actual opposing sword: Balaam wishes for a sword to kill the donkey; the revelation then exposes the angel’s drawn sword and statement that Balaam, rather than the animal, would have been killed without her avoidance.

Numbers 22:30
And the donkey says to Balaam: Am I not your donkey, which you have ridden from your youth until this day? Have I acted like this toward you out of deliberate disregard? He said: No.
  Numbers 22:32-33 — Same-chapter donkey’s defence and the angel’s explanation: The donkey asks whether her behaviour was deliberate disregard, which Balaam denies; the angel then challenges the beating and explains that her avoidance preserved Balaam’s life.

Numbers 22:31
God uncovered Balaam's eyes, and he sees the Lord's angel opposing him in the road with his sword drawn in his hand. He bent down and prostrated himself on his face.
  1 Chronicles 21:16 — Related vision of a drawn-sword angel and prostration: David also sees the Lord’s angel with a drawn sword and falls on his face with the elders; Balaam here sees the opposing angel after his eyes are uncovered and prostrates himself.
  Numbers 22:23 — Same-chapter revealed angel was already visible to the donkey: Balaam’s opened eyes reveal the opposing angel with a drawn sword; the first road encounter already described the donkey seeing that drawn-sword opposition before Balaam understood it.

Numbers 22:32
And God's angel said to him: Why have you struck your donkey this third time? Behold, I have come out to oppose you, because your way is not fitting before me.
  Numbers 22:23-27 — Same-chapter rebuke concerns the three narrated blows: The angel expressly asks about striking the donkey a third time; the earlier sequence supplies her three encounters and Balaam’s respective blows, without replacing the angel’s stated judgment about the way.

Numbers 22:33
And the donkey saw me and turned away from me this third time. If she had not turned away, I would now have killed you and preserved her.
  Numbers 22:23-27 — Same-chapter protective avoidance explained after the encounters: The angel explains that the donkey’s avoidance saved Balaam while she would have been preserved; the preceding scenes give the differing road, wall, and crouching responses whose significance Balaam had missed.

Numbers 22:34
And Balaam said unto the Lord's angel: I have sinned, for I did not know that you were opposing me in the road. Now, if this does not please you, I will turn back.
  Numbers 22:31-33 — Same-chapter confession follows the revealed danger and rebuke: Balaam admits sin and lack of knowledge after his eyes are opened and the angel explains the danger and the donkey’s protection; he then offers to turn back if required.

Numbers 22:35
And God's angel said to Balaam: Go with the men, but take care to speak the word that I tell you. Balaam went with Balak's officials.
  Numbers 22:20 — Same-chapter renewed travel permission and word constraint: The angel permits Balaam to go with the men but requires the specified speech; the earlier night instruction likewise couples permission to follow with doing what God says.

Numbers 22:36
And Balak heard that Balaam had arrived and went out to meet him at a city of Moab on the Arnon border, at the edge of the territory.
  Numbers 21:13 — Arnon frontier provides the stated meeting’s geographical context: Balak meets Balaam at a Moabite city on the Arnon border; the earlier itinerary explicitly identifies the Arnon as the border between Moab and the Amorites, without naming the meeting city.

Numbers 22:37
And Balak said to Balaam: Did I not send to summon you? Why did you not come to me? Am I really unable to honour you?
  Numbers 22:16-17 — Same-chapter complaint recalls the explicit summons and honour offer: Balak questions why Balaam did not come and whether he can honour him; his second embassy had explicitly urged Balaam to come and promised rich honour.

Numbers 22:38
And Balaam said to Balak: Behold, I have come unto you. Am I now able to say anything? I shall speak the word that God puts in my mouth.
  Numbers 23:5 — Declared speech limitation and the later placed word: Balaam says he can speak only what God puts in his mouth; the later consultation expressly has God place a word there and order his return to speak it.

Numbers 22:39
And Balaam went with Balak, and they came to towns of farmsteads.
  Numbers 24:25 — Joint journey and the eventual separation of the named pair: Balaam goes with Balak here during their reception journey; after the oracles Balaam returns to his own place and Balak goes home, closing the named pair’s time together without replacing the farmstead-town wording.

Numbers 22:40
And Balak sacrificed sheep and calves and sent portions to Balaam and the officials with him.
  Deuteronomy 23:5 — Contrasting food provision to Balaam and recalled treatment of Israel: Balak supplies sheep-and-calf portions to Balaam and his officials; the later law recalls Moab’s failure to meet Israel with bread and water and its hiring of Balaam, preserving the different recipients and foods.

Numbers 22:41
And in the morning Balak took Balaam and brought him up to Baal's pillar. From there he showed him a part of the people.
  Numbers 23:13 — Balak repeatedly arranges a limited view of Israel: Balak first shows Balaam part of the people from Baal’s pillar; he later requests another place with only a partial view for cursing, without identifying the two sites as the same place.

Numbers 23:1
And Balaam said to Balak: Build me seven altars here, and prepare seven calves and seven rams for me here.
  Numbers 23:29 — Same-chapter repeated seven-altar preparation instruction: The later instruction repeats seven altars, seven calves, and seven rams, the same specified quantities and offering types that Balaam requests at this first site.

Numbers 23:2
And Balak did as Balaam told him, and he offered a calf and a ram on each altar.
  Numbers 23:1 — Same-chapter preparation command and its execution: Balak acts on Balaam’s immediately preceding demand for seven altars with calves and rams; the report specifies a calf and ram offered on each altar.
  Numbers 23:30 — Same-chapter corresponding execution at another offering site: Both execution reports say Balak does what Balaam requests and offers a calf and ram on each altar, preserving the repeated procedure at the distinct sites.

Numbers 23:3
And Balaam said to Balak: Stand beside your sacrifice, while I go to see whether God will appear to meet me. Whatever He shows me, I will tell you. Balak stood beside his sacrifice, and Balaam went to inquire of God; he went straight on.
  Numbers 23:4-6 — Same-chapter proposed consultation and its narrated result: Balaam leaves Balak beside the sacrifice to seek God’s appearance and report His word; God appears, puts a word in his mouth, and Balaam returns to the waiting king.

Numbers 23:4
And God appeared to Balaam, and Balaam said to Him: I have prepared the seven altars and offered a calf and a ram on each altar.
  Numbers 23:1-2 — Same-chapter reported altar count matches the preparation: Balaam reports preparing seven altars and offering a calf and ram on each; the earlier demand and execution supply those same specific quantities and animals.

Numbers 23:5
And God put a word in Balaam's mouth and said: Return to Balak and thus you shall speak.
  Numbers 22:38 — Placed divine word implements Balaam’s declared speech limit: Balaam had told Balak he would speak the word God put in his mouth; this encounter expressly narrates God putting that word there and directing what to say.
  Numbers 23:16 — Same-chapter repeated word-placement and return command: The second encounter likewise has God meet Balaam, put a word in his mouth, and send him back to Balak with specified speech, repeating this particular consultation procedure.

Numbers 23:6
And he returned to him, and there he stood beside his whole burnt offerings, with all the officials of Moab.
  Numbers 23:3 — Same-chapter return finds Balak at the appointed sacrifice: Balaam returns to Balak standing beside the offerings with Moab’s officials; the preceding instruction told Balak to stand by his sacrifice while Balaam sought God’s answer.

Numbers 23:7
And God's Spirit came upon him, and he took up his parable and said: Balak summoned me from Mesopotamia, the king of Moab from the eastern mountains: Come, curse Jacob for me; come, call down a curse on Israel for me.
  Numbers 22:5-6 — Earlier commission is voiced within the oracle: The oracle quotes Balak’s summons to curse Jacob and Israel; the earlier embassy records Balak summoning Balaam and asking him to curse the people who came from Egypt.
  Deuteronomy 23:5 — Later recital likewise places Balaam’s curse commission in Mesopotamia: The oracle says Balak summoned the speaker from Mesopotamia to curse Israel; Deuteronomy explicitly names Balaam son of Beor from Mesopotamia in its recollection of hiring him against Israel.

Numbers 23:8
Why should I curse whom the Lord does not curse? Why should I call down a curse on whom God does not curse?
  Numbers 22:12 — Oracle’s refusal follows God’s express prohibition: Balaam’s question about cursing those God does not curse corresponds to the earlier divine command not to curse the people because they are blessed.
  Deuteronomy 23:6 — Later recollection of God turning the intended curse to blessing: The law recalls God refusing Balaam and turning curses into blessing for Israel; this oracle rejects the very curse Balak requested because God does not curse them.

Numbers 23:9
From the tops of mountains I shall see them, and from the hills I shall observe them. Behold, a people who will dwell alone, not counted among the nations.
  Deuteronomy 33:28 — Israel dwelling alone in the later blessing: The later blessing likewise describes Israel dwelling alone upon Jacob’s land, corresponding to the oracle’s distinct people dwelling alone and not counted among the nations.

Numbers 23:10
Who has measured Jacob’s seed exactly, and who will count Israel's clans? May my soul die among righteous souls, and may my seed be like their seed!
  Genesis 28:13-14 — Earlier promise of Jacob’s innumerable and spreading seed: God’s promise to Jacob compares his seed with the earth’s sand and its spread in every direction; this oracle asks who can measure Jacob’s seed or count Israel’s clans.
  Hosea 2:1 — Later unmeasurable-number promise for Israel’s sons: Hosea compares the number of Israel’s sons with sea sand that cannot be measured or counted; this oracle likewise asks who can measure Jacob’s seed or count Israel’s clans, preserving the actual Hosea2:1 source division.

Numbers 23:11
And Balak said to Balaam: What have you done to me? I summoned you to curse my enemies, and look, you have pronounced a blessing!
  Numbers 23:7-10 — Same-chapter objection concerns the just-spoken oracle: Balak objects that blessing has replaced his requested curse; the preceding oracle explicitly recounts his curse demand and answers it with Israel’s distinctive and abundant condition.

Numbers 23:12
And Balaam said to Balak: Must I not take care to speak what God puts in my mouth?
  Numbers 23:5 — Same-chapter speech defence recalls the word actually supplied: Balaam says he must speak what God puts in his mouth; the preceding encounter specifically narrates that placement and the order to return to Balak with the word.

Numbers 23:13
And Balak said to him: Come with me to another place, from which you shall not see them except for a part; you shall in no wise see them all. Curse them for me from there.
  Numbers 22:41 — Second partial-view arrangement after the first vantage: Balak again arranges for Balaam to see only part of Israel and asks for a curse; the earlier scene had shown him part of the people from Baal’s pillar, without equating the sites.

Numbers 23:14
And he took him to a lookout in a field, to the summit of the Hewn Height, built seven altars there, and offered a calf and a ram on each altar.
  Numbers 23:1-2 — Same-chapter repeated altar and animal procedure at a new site: The lookout at the Hewn Height receives seven altars and a calf and ram on each, repeating the first preparation while retaining this different stated vantage.

Numbers 23:15
And Balaam said to Balak: Stand beside your sacrifice, while I go to inquire of God.
  Numbers 23:3 — Same-chapter renewed instruction to wait beside the sacrifice: Balaam again tells Balak to stand by his sacrifice while he inquires of God, repeating the earlier waiting-and-consulting arrangement without supplying another method of divination.

Numbers 23:16
And God met Balaam, put a word in his mouth, and said: Return to Balak and speak these words.
  Numbers 23:5 — Same-chapter corresponding divine word and return instructions: Both encounters expressly describe God putting a word in Balaam’s mouth and commanding him to return and speak to Balak, identifying the source of each announced oracle.

Numbers 23:17
And he returned to him, and there he stood beside his whole burning, with all the officials of Moab. Balak said to him: What did the Lord say?
  Numbers 23:15-16 — Same-chapter waiting arrangement and inquiry completed: Balaam returns to Balak waiting with the officials beside his offering after the commanded consultation; Balak’s question asks for the Lord’s answer that the preceding encounter supplied.

Numbers 23:18
And he took up his parable and said: Rise, Balak, and hear! Listen as a witness, son of Zippor!
  Numbers 23:19-24 — Same-chapter summons to hear and its particular oracle content: The address calls Balak son of Zippor to hear; the following oracle gives the specific divine reliability, irreversible blessing, presence, exodus, divination, and lion imagery being announced.

Numbers 23:19
God is not as a man, to be led astray, nor as a son of man, to be threatened; having spoken, shall He not act? Shall He speak, and not abide by it?
  1 Samuel 15:29 — Related divine-human contrast with different stated predicates: Both speeches contrast God with a human being; this oracle denies being led astray or threatened and affirms His spoken word, while Samuel’s statement denies turning back or changing His mind.
  Numbers 23:20 — Same-chapter reliability declaration and unrevoked blessing: The oracle asks whether God will fail to act on His word; the following blessing declaration applies that reliability by insisting that the speaker will bless and not reverse it.

Numbers 23:20
Behold, I have been taken up to bless. I will bless and certainly will not reverse it.
  Deuteronomy 23:6 — Later remembered transformation of the requested curse: Deuteronomy recalls God turning the proposed curse into blessing because He loved Israel; this oracle explicitly declares blessing and refuses its reversal, despite Balak’s hostile commission.

Numbers 23:21
There shall be no toil in Jacob, neither shall distress be seen in Israel; the Lord his God is with him; the glories of rulers are in him.
  Numbers 14:8-9 — The Lord’s presence with Israel in another land-conflict appeal: Joshua and Caleb tell Israel not to fear the land’s people because the Lord is among them; this oracle likewise places the Lord with Israel, retaining its own no-toil and rulers’ glory wording.

Numbers 23:22
God brought them out of Egypt; his glory is as that of a one-horned beast.
  Numbers 24:8 — Exodus and one-horned-beast imagery repeated in the next oracle: Both oracles join God’s bringing Israel from Egypt with glory like a one-horned beast; the later oracle adds enemy-devouring and battle imagery rather than replacing the beast with another familiar rendering.
  Deuteronomy 33:17 — Related one-horned-animal image in a distinct tribal blessing: Joseph’s blessing compares his horns with those of a one-horned animal and describes goring nations; this oracle uses the related beast image for the people brought from Egypt, preserving the different stated scope.

Numbers 23:23
There is no omen-taking in Jacob and no divination in Israel. At the appointed time it shall be said to Jacob and Israel what God will accomplish.
  Deuteronomy 18:10-14 — Oracle’s exclusion of divination beside the explicit prohibition: The law forbids divination and omens among Israel and contrasts the nations’ practices; this oracle likewise declares no omen-taking in Jacob or divination in Israel, without changing its predictive form into a legal command.

Numbers 23:24
Behold, a people will rise like a lion cub and exult like a lion. It will not sleep until it eats prey and drinks the blood of the slain.
  Genesis 49:9 — Related lion and lion-cub imagery with different actions: Jacob’s Judah blessing pictures a reclining lion and cub and asks who will rouse him; this oracle pictures the people rising like a cub and lion and not sleeping until prey is consumed.

Numbers 23:25
And Balak said to Balaam: Neither curse them for me nor bless them at all!
  Numbers 23:11 — Same-chapter objection changes after repeated blessing: Balak earlier protested receiving blessing when he requested a curse; he now demands neither curse nor blessing, preserving the changed wording of his response to Balaam’s oracles.

Numbers 23:26
And Balaam answered Balak: Did I not tell you that I must do the word that God speaks?
  Numbers 23:12 — Same-chapter Balaam repeats the stated divine-word constraint: Balaam again insists he must do God’s word; his earlier answer likewise said he must take care to speak what God puts in his mouth, addressing the same king’s objection.

Numbers 23:27
And Balak said to Balaam: Come; I will take you to another place. If it pleases God, curse them for me from there.
  Numbers 23:13 — Same-chapter another proposed move seeks the same curse outcome: Balak again proposes taking Balaam to another location to obtain a curse; the earlier move likewise aimed at cursing from a partial view, while this request adds if it pleases God.

Numbers 23:28
And Balak took Balaam to the summit of Peor, which extends toward the wilderness.
  Numbers 23:27 — Same-chapter proposed alternative site and the named destination: Balak proposes moving Balaam to another place for a curse; the next report identifies that destination as Peor’s summit toward the wilderness, without substituting another familiar site-name.

Numbers 23:29
And Balaam said to Balak: Build me seven altars here, and prepare seven calves and seven rams for me here.
  Numbers 23:1 — Same-chapter seven-altar demand repeated for the third setting: The command repeats the first site’s seven altars, seven calves, and seven rams, retaining the exact quantities and animals rather than supplying a different sacrificial scheme.

Numbers 23:30
And Balak did as Balaam told him and offered a calf and a ram on each altar.
  Numbers 23:29 — Same-chapter final preparation command and execution: Balak’s offering a calf and ram on each altar carries out Balaam’s immediately preceding demand for seven altars and the matching seven calves and rams.
  Numbers 23:2 — Same-chapter first and later executions of the same altar procedure: Both reports say Balak acts as Balaam tells him and offers a calf and ram on every altar, tying the distinct settings to the repeated specified procedure.

Numbers 24:1
And Balaam saw that it was good before the Lord to bless Israel, so he did not go as usual to seek omens. He turned his face toward the wilderness.
  Numbers 23:20 — Recognition of blessing beside the preceding blessing oracle: Balaam sees that blessing Israel is good before the Lord and stops his usual omen-seeking; the preceding oracle had expressly declared blessing that would not be reversed, without supplying an unstated motive for his earlier practice.

Numbers 24:2
And Balaam raised his eyes and sees Israel encamped by tribes, and God's Spirit came within him.
  Numbers 23:7 — Repeated divine Spirit upon Balaam before an oracle: Both scenes explicitly place God’s Spirit upon or within Balaam before he delivers a parable, retaining the distinct descriptions rather than attributing the oracle solely to his divination.

Numbers 24:3
And he took up his parable and said: Balaam son of Beor declares; the man who sees truly declares,
  Numbers 24:15 — Same-chapter repeated named oracle introduction: The later introduction repeats Balaam son of Beor and the man who sees truly, matching this named speaker and sight declaration without replacing its supplied wording.

Numbers 24:4
one who hears God's oracles declares, who saw a vision of God in sleep, with his eyes uncovered:
  Numbers 12:6 — Vision and sleep as stated prophetic revelation: The earlier divine speech describes making Himself known to a prophet in vision and speaking in sleep; Balaam’s oracle introduction uses those same modes, retaining its own uncovered-eyes description.
  Numbers 24:16 — Same-chapter repeated vision-and-hearing introduction: The later opening likewise describes hearing God’s oracles, seeing a divine vision in sleep, and uncovered eyes, while adding knowledge from the Most High to this introduction’s terms.

Numbers 24:5
How beautiful are your houses, Jacob, your tents, Israel!
  Numbers 24:2 — Same-chapter beautiful tents describe the observed encampment: Balaam first sees Israel encamped by tribes; his ensuing parable addresses Jacob’s houses and Israel’s tents as beautiful, supplying the particular scene being praised.

Numbers 24:6
Like shaded wooded valleys, like gardens beside rivers, like tents pitched by the Lord, like cedars beside waters.
  Numbers 24:5 — Same-chapter landscape similes expand the tents’ praise: The preceding line names the beautiful houses and tents of Jacob and Israel; these wooded-valley, river-garden, Lord-pitched-tent, and cedar comparisons expand that specific praise without declaring literal botanical locations.

Numbers 24:7
A man shall come forth from his seed and rule over many nations. His kingdom shall rise higher than Gog, and his kingdom shall increase.
  Numbers 24:17 — Same-chapter rising human figure and extended dominion language: Both oracles speak of a man coming from Israel’s seed or arising from Israel and gaining power; this line names kingdom growth above Gog, while the later line names Moab and Seth, without substituting Agag or asserting a uniquely resolved historical ruler.

Numbers 24:8
God guided him out of Egypt; his glory is like a one-horned beast's. He shall devour the nations of his enemies, suck the marrow from their fat parts, and shoot down an enemy with his missiles.
  Numbers 23:22 — Egyptian deliverance and one-horned-beast glory repeated: The earlier oracle likewise joins God’s bringing the people from Egypt with one-horned-beast glory; this line expands that combination with devouring enemy nations and particular battle imagery.

Numbers 24:9
He lay down and rested like a lion, like a lion cub—who will rouse him? Those who bless you are blessed, and those who curse you are cursed.
  Genesis 49:9 — Closely matching reclining lion and cub comparison: Judah’s blessing likewise describes reclining and sleeping like a lion and cub and asks who will rouse him, closely corresponding to this oracle’s resting lion image.
  Genesis 27:29 — Matching blessing-and-curse reciprocity in Jacob’s earlier blessing: Isaac’s blessing likewise declares the curser cursed and the blesser blessed; this oracle applies that same reciprocal blessing-and-curse language after its lion comparison.

Numbers 24:10
And Balak became angry with Balaam and clapped his hands. He said to Balaam: I summoned you to curse my enemy, and look, you have thoroughly blessed them this third time!
  Numbers 23:11 — Balak’s repeated objection to blessing instead of cursing: Balak earlier protested that Balaam blessed the enemies he was summoned to curse; here his anger names the same reversal and expressly describes the blessing as a third occurrence.

Numbers 24:11
Now flee to your own place! I said I would honour you, but now the Lord has deprived you of glory.
  Numbers 22:17 — Earlier promised honour recalled in Balak’s dismissal: Balak had promised rich honour for coming to curse; he now orders Balaam away and says the Lord has deprived him of glory, preserving that explanation as Balak’s statement.

Numbers 24:12
And Balaam said to Balak: Did I not tell the messengers whom you sent to me:
  Numbers 22:18 — Balaam expressly recalls his answer to the messengers: The question about what he told Balak’s messengers points back to the earlier answer about a house full of silver and gold and the inability to transgress the Lord’s word.

Numbers 24:13
Even if Balak gave me his house full of silver and gold, I could not transgress the Lord's word to do evil or good on my own. Whatever God says, I must say.
  Numbers 22:18 — Repeated houseful-of-wealth and divine-word limitation: Balaam repeats his earlier declaration that even Balak’s house full of silver and gold cannot authorize transgressing God’s word, preserving this reply’s own evil-or-good and must-say expressions.

Numbers 24:14
And now look, I am returning to my place. Come, I will advise you what this people will do to your people at the end of the days.
  Numbers 24:17 — Same-chapter announced future advice and its Moab-directed content: Balaam announces what Israel will do to Balak’s people at the end of days; the following oracle specifically describes an arising Israelite figure crushing Moab’s leaders, retaining its not-now qualification.

Numbers 24:15
And he took up his parable and said: Balaam son of Beor declares; the man who sees truly declares,
  Numbers 24:3 — Same-chapter identical Balaam-and-sight preamble repeated: Both introductions name Balaam son of Beor and the man who sees truly, establishing the repeated speaker formula before distinct portions of his oracles.

Numbers 24:16
one who hears God's oracles, who knows knowledge from the Most High and has seen a vision of God in sleep, with his eyes uncovered:
  Numbers 24:4 — Same-chapter repeated revealed-hearing and vision language: The preceding oracle introduction likewise names hearing God’s oracles and seeing a divine vision in sleep with eyes uncovered; this opening additionally states knowledge from the Most High.

Numbers 24:17
I will show him, but not now; I pronounce him blessed, but he does not draw near. A star shall rise from Jacob, and a man shall arise from Israel. He shall crush Moab's leaders and plunder all the sons of Seth.
  Matthew 2:1-2 — Later star-and-royal-figure narrative with distinct stated claims: Matthew names Jesus’ birth and the magi’s report of the star of the one born King of the Jews; this oracle joins Jacob’s rising star with an arising Israelite man, without Matthew explicitly quoting Numbers or naming it as his unique source.
  Numbers 24:14 — Same-chapter Moab prophecy follows the announced future advice: The crushing of Moab’s leaders supplies a particular outcome concerning Balak’s people after Balaam announces what Israel will do to them; the oracle expressly places the sight beyond the immediate present.

Numbers 24:18
And Edom shall become an inheritance, and Esau, his enemy, shall become an inheritance; Israel acted with strength.
  Genesis 36:8 — Explicit Esau-Edom identification behind the paired names: Genesis explicitly states that Esau is Edom; this oracle names Edom and Esau together as prospective inheritance, without requiring a new conjectural equivalence between different place spellings.

Numbers 24:19
And one shall arise from Jacob and destroy whoever escapes from a city.
  Numbers 24:17 — Same-chapter arising-from-Jacob and conquest language: The preceding oracle likewise joins an arising Israelite figure with military domination; this line repeats arising from Jacob and destruction of a city’s escapees, without identifying the unnamed city or independently resolving the figure’s historical identity.

Numbers 24:20
And he saw Amalek, took up his parable, and said: Amalek is the beginning of nations, and their seed shall perish.
  Exodus 17:14-16 — Earlier announced judgment against Amalek’s remembrance: The earlier declaration promises to blot out Amalek’s remembrance and describes continuing conflict; this oracle likewise announces the perishing of Amalek’s seed, retaining its separate beginning-of-nations description.

Numbers 24:21
And he saw the Kenite, took up his parable, and said: Your dwelling is strong; even if you set your nest in a rock,
  Numbers 24:22 — Same-chapter secure-nest image and the following captivity warning: The Kenite oracle pictures a strong dwelling and rock-set nest; the following line continues nest imagery but names Beor and Assyrian captivity, preserving those names without declaring Beor and the Kenite identical.

Numbers 24:22
And even if a nest of craftiness should belong to Beor, Assyrians shall take you captive.
  Numbers 24:21 — Same-chapter continued nest imagery with distinct supplied names: The preceding oracle depicts the Kenite’s rock-set nest; this conditional line again names a nest but specifies Beor and Assyrian captivity, relating the imagery without silently substituting one named subject for another.

Numbers 24:23
And he saw Og, took up his parable, and said: Alas, alas! Who will live when God brings these things about?
  Numbers 24:24 — Same-chapter survival lament and the subsequent affliction announcement: The lament asks who will live when God brings these things about; the following line names a coming one from the Kitians, affliction of Asshur and the Hebrews, and shared perishing, without replacing the stated sight of Og.

Numbers 24:24
And one shall come forth from the hand of the Kitians, and they shall afflict Asshur and the Hebrews; they themselves shall perish together.
  Numbers 24:23 — Same-chapter affliction oracle gives content after the survival question: The coming afflicter, the named affected peoples, and perishing follow Balaam’s question about survival when God brings events about; this relation does not equate Kitians with a differently named people elsewhere.

Numbers 24:25
And Balaam rose and departed, returning to his place, and Balak went home.
  Numbers 24:14 — Same-chapter announced return and the eventual departure: Balaam earlier announces his return to his own place before further advice; the closing report explicitly narrates that return and Balak’s separate journey home.

Numbers 25:1
And Israel lodged at Shittim, and the people profaned themselves by engaging in sexual immorality with the daughters of Moab.
  Numbers 31:16 — Later explanation of the women’s part in the Peor offence: The later speech says women, according to Balaam’s word, caused Israel’s turning from the Lord because of Peor; this opening records sexual immorality with Moab’s daughters without itself naming Balaam’s counsel.
  1 Corinthians 10:8 — Later wilderness sexual-immorality warning with its own count: Paul warns from earlier sexual immorality and says twenty-three thousand fell in one day; this episode records Israel’s sexual immorality, while its later total is twenty-four thousand, so neither number or timeframe is made identical.

Numbers 25:2
And they invited them to the sacrifices of their idols. The people ate from their sacrifices and bowed down to their idols.
  Psalms 105:28-29 — Later recollection of Baal-peor meals and provocation: The psalm likewise joins initiation to Baal-peor with eating sacrifices and provocation; this account specifies the invitations, eating, and bowing to idols without replacing idols’ sacrifices with a differently stated source phrase.

Numbers 25:3
And Israel was initiated into the worship of Baal of Peor, and the Lord became angry with Israel.
  Deuteronomy 4:3-4 — Later direct recollection of Baal-peor’s destruction and survivors: Deuteronomy explicitly recalls what the Lord did at Baal-peor, destroying followers while those cleaving to Him survived; this account names Israel’s initiation and the Lord’s anger.
  Psalms 105:28-29 — Baal-peor initiation and anger remembered in the psalm: The psalm explicitly names initiation to Baal-peor and ensuing provocation and falling; this verse supplies the same named worship and divine anger before the plague is narrated.

Numbers 25:4

  Numbers 25:11 — Same-chapter anger-turning direction and the later stated restraint: The command seeks to turn the Lord’s fierce anger away through a public example of leaders; the later declaration credits Phinehas with stopping wrath, retaining that distinct narrated agent rather than claiming the prescribed public act is separately reported as performed.

Numbers 25:5
And Moses said to Israel's tribes: Each of you shall kill his own kinsman who was initiated into the worship of Baal of Peor.
  Numbers 25:3-4 — Same-chapter tribal killing instruction responds to the named initiation: Moses directs each tribe to kill its own kinsman initiated into Baal of Peor; the preceding report identifies that initiation and the Lord’s anger and public-example command, preserving the distinct wording and recipients.

Numbers 25:6
And an Israelite man came and brought his brother to the Midianite woman before Moses and the whole congregation of the sons of Israel, while they were weeping at the entrance of the Tent of Testimony.
  Numbers 25:7-8 — Same-chapter public incident and Phinehas’ narrated response: The public arrival involving an Israelite man, his brother, and the Midianite woman is followed by Phinehas taking a lance and killing a man and woman; the comparison does not decide which of the two males mentioned here is the later victim.

Numbers 25:7
And Phinehas son of Eleazar, son of Aaron the priest, saw it, rose from among the congregation, and took a lance in his hand.
  Sirach 45:23 — Later remembrance names Phinehas son of Eleazar and his intervention: Sirach explicitly names Phinehas son of Eleazar standing firm when the people turned away and making atonement; this scene gives that same patronymic and additionally his Aaronic priestly ancestry as he rises with a lance.
  Numbers 31:6 — Phinehas’ priestly ancestry repeated in the Midian campaign: The later military report again names Phinehas son of Eleazar, son of Aaron the priest, preserving the exact ancestry given here before his intervention in the Peor crisis.

Numbers 25:8
He went after the Israelite man into the chamber and stabbed both of them, the Israelite man and the woman, through her womb. The plague against the sons of Israel stopped.
  Psalms 105:30 — Later psalmic account of Phinehas stopping the affliction: The psalm explicitly says Phinehas stood, made atonement, and the breaking ceased; this narrative reports the killings and the plague stopping, preserving the respective descriptions without supplying a new procedure.
  Numbers 25:14-15 — Same-chapter named notices identify the struck-down man and woman: The later notices identify the man struck with the Midianite woman as Zimri and the woman as Cozbi, supplying the names and family positions of this scene’s otherwise unnamed victims without resolving the earlier brother wording.

Numbers 25:9
And those who died in the plague numbered twenty-four thousand.
  1 Corinthians 10:8 — Different recorded numbers in the later warning: Numbers gives twenty-four thousand dead in the plague; Paul’s sexual-immorality warning says twenty-three thousand in one day, so the comparison preserves the different totals and the target’s additional timeframe without an invented reconciliation.
  Numbers 26:1 — Following census begins after the plague: The following census introduction expressly places the Lord’s speech after the plague; this account supplies its reported death total and the Peor setting, without treating the census as part of that death count.

Numbers 25:10
And the Lord spoke unto Moses, saying:
  Numbers 25:11-13 — Same-chapter speech introduction and the covenant declaration: This speech introduces the Lord’s specific assessment of Phinehas’ zeal, the restraint of wrath, and the promised covenant of peace and enduring priesthood for him and his seed.

Numbers 25:11
Phinehas son of Eleazar, son of Aaron the priest, has stopped My wrath against the sons of Israel by being zealous with My zeal among them, and I did not consume the sons of Israel in My zeal.
  Sirach 45:23-24 — Later recollection of Phinehas’ zeal, atonement, and covenant: Sirach likewise names Phinehas’ zeal and atonement during the people’s turning away and follows them with the covenant of peace and enduring priesthood, corresponding to this divine explanation and its continuation.
  Psalms 105:30-31 — Later assessment of Phinehas’ effective intervention: The psalm says Phinehas’ atonement stopped the breaking and was reckoned as righteousness; this declaration says his zeal stopped divine wrath and prevented Israel’s consumption, retaining the distinct evaluative terms.

Numbers 25:12

  Sirach 45:24 — Explicit later remembrance of Phinehas’ covenant of peace: Sirach expressly recalls a covenant of peace established for Phinehas and links it with the priesthood of his offspring, repeating the particular covenant given here.
  Numbers 25:13 — Same-chapter covenant announcement and its enduring priestly scope: The gift of a covenant of peace is followed immediately by its specification as an everlasting covenant of priesthood for Phinehas and his seed, with the stated reason of zeal and atonement.

Numbers 25:13

  Sirach 45:23-24 — Later atonement and hereditary priesthood account: Sirach likewise connects Phinehas’ atonement with a peace covenant and priestly majesty for his offspring forever, corresponding to this explicit explanation of zeal and enduring priesthood.

Numbers 25:14

  Numbers 25:8 — Same-chapter man’s name supplied after the death scene: The notice identifies the struck-down Israelite as Zimri son of Salu, a Simeonite ancestral ruler; the earlier scene supplies the man-and-woman killing to which that identification refers.

Numbers 25:15

  Numbers 25:18 — Same-chapter Cozbi’s family and death recalled in the Midian charge: The later explanation again names Cozbi as a Midianite ruler’s daughter struck on the plague day, recalling this named woman and her family position without assuming that Zur is identical with another occurrence of that name.

Numbers 25:16
And the Lord spoke unto Moses, saying: Speak to the sons of Israel, saying:
  Numbers 25:17-18 — Same-chapter speech introduction and specific Midian command: The speech directs Moses to address Israel; the following lines provide its particular command of hostility and its stated reasons involving Peor and Cozbi.

Numbers 25:17
Be hostile to the Midianites and strike them,
  Numbers 31:2-3 — Later command of vengeance against Midian: The later speech explicitly orders vengeance for Israel from Midian and Moses equips men for battle; this earlier instruction commands hostility and striking, with its own reasons given in the next verse.

Numbers 25:18
for they are hostile to you through the deceit by which they deceive you because of Peor and because of Cozbi, daughter of a Midianite ruler, their sister, who was struck down on the day of the plague because of Peor.
  Numbers 31:16 — Later explanation again ties the Midian women and plague to Peor: Moses later says the women caused Israel to disregard the Lord because of Peor and the plague followed; this explanation names Midianite deceit, Peor, and Cozbi’s plague-day death without treating both explanations as identical wording.

Numbers 26:1

  Numbers 25:8-9 — Census follows the named plague: The census is introduced after the plague, whose cessation and twenty-four thousand deaths are reported in the preceding account.

Numbers 26:2

Numbers 26:3

  Numbers 26:63 — Same-chapter location and registrars restated: The concluding notice repeats Moses and Eleazar as registrars in Araboth of Moab beside the Jordan opposite Jericho.

Numbers 26:4

  Numbers 26:2 — Same-chapter age instruction implemented: The twenty-year threshold reported by Moses and Eleazar repeats the specific age qualification ordered for this registration.

Numbers 26:52

  Numbers 26:53-56 — Same-chapter speech introduces land allocation: This speech introduces inheritance by registered numbers and ancestral tribes, with proportional size and division by lot both explicitly required.

  Numbers 33:54 — Registration and tribal land allocation: Both instructions connect inheritance with tribal groups, their relative numbers, and the allocation of the land among them.

Numbers 26:54

  Numbers 33:54 — Proportionate inheritance rule repeated: Both instructions increase the possession of larger groups and decrease that of smaller groups, preserving the registration-based principle.

Numbers 26:55

Numbers 26:56

Numbers 26:62

Numbers 27:1

  Numbers 27:4-5 — Same-chapter hearing and particular case: Their appearance before Moses, Eleazar, the rulers, and the congregation introduces the inheritance request that Moses brings before the Lord.

Numbers 27:3
Our father died in the wilderness. He was not among the congregation that rose against the Lord in Korah's congregation; he died because of his own sin, and he had no sons.
  Numbers 26:37 — Sonless father's household identified: The daughters state that their father had no sons, the precise household circumstance already recorded in Zelophehad's census notice.
  Numbers 26:9-11 — Korah affiliation expressly disclaimed: The petition distinguishes Zelophehad from Korah's congregation, whose participants and judgment the preceding census specifically recalls, without identifying his own sin.

Numbers 27:4
Let our father's name not be erased from his clan because he has no son. Give us a possession among our father's brothers.
  Numbers 27:7 — Same-chapter requested possession granted: The daughters ask for a possession among their father's brothers, and the ruling expressly grants their father's allotment among those brethren.

Numbers 27:5

  Numbers 27:6-8 — Same-chapter referred case receives ruling: Moses brings the daughters' case before the Lord, whose answer grants their possession and establishes the daughter-inheritance rule.

Numbers 27:6
And the Lord spoke unto Moses, saying:
  Numbers 27:7-11 — Same-chapter speech introduces inheritance judgment: This speech introduces approval of the daughters' claim and the stated sequence for assigning a sonless deceased man's inheritance.

Numbers 27:7

  Numbers 36:2 — Inheritance ruling explicitly recalled: The later tribal appeal expressly recalls the Lord's instruction to give Zelophehad's inheritance to his daughters.
  Joshua 17:4-6 — Daughters receive the promised allotment: The later account records the daughters invoking God's command through Moses and receiving a lot among their father's brothers.

Numbers 27:8

  Numbers 36:8 — Inheriting daughter and tribal continuity: The rule giving inheritance to a daughter when no son survives is developed by the later clan-marriage rule for daughters who inherit.

Numbers 27:9

  Numbers 27:8 — Same-chapter next contingency after daughter: The instruction to assign inheritance to a brother applies when there is no daughter, following the preceding sonless man's daughter rule.

Numbers 27:10

  Numbers 27:9 — Same-chapter next contingency after brothers: The father's brother receives the inheritance only under the stated absence of brothers, continuing the preceding brother-inheritance contingency.

Numbers 27:11

  Numbers 27:8-10 — Same-chapter final relative after specified kin: The nearest tribal relative is reached only after the stated son, daughter, brother, and father's-brother contingencies, completing the rule of judgment.

Numbers 27:12

  Deuteronomy 34:1-4 — Nebo viewing command carried out: The command names Mount Nebo and viewing Canaan; Deuteronomy narrates Moses ascending Nebo and seeing the land he will not enter.

Numbers 27:13

  Numbers 20:25-28 — Aaron's death supplies the comparison: Moses' coming death is compared with Aaron's gathering at Mount Hor, whose ascent, vestment transfer, and death are previously narrated.
  Deuteronomy 34:4-5 — Viewing followed by Moses' death: Deuteronomy records Moses being shown the land without entering and then dying, matching the announced sequence here.

Numbers 27:14

  Numbers 20:12 — Failure to sanctify at the water recalled: Both judgments explain Moses and Aaron's exclusion by failure to show the Lord holy before the congregation at the water incident.

Numbers 27:15

  Numbers 27:16-17 — Same-chapter prayer introduction: Moses' speech introduces his particular request that God appoint a leader to guide the congregation rather than leave it shepherdless.

Numbers 27:16

  Numbers 16:22 — Distinctive divine title recalled: Moses again addresses the God of spirits and all flesh, the distinctive title used in the earlier plea concerning the congregation.

Numbers 27:17

  Deuteronomy 34:9 — Joshua's Spirit and hand-laying recalled: Deuteronomy identifies Joshua's spirit of understanding in connection with Moses laying hands upon him, recalling the succession instruction here.

  Numbers 27:22 — Same-chapter public presentation command fulfilled: Moses sets Joshua before Eleazar and the congregation, carrying out the instruction for his publicly witnessed installation.

Numbers 27:20

Numbers 27:21

  Exodus 28:30 — Priestly judgment through manifestations: Joshua's consultation before Eleazar corresponds to the oracle-piece bearing Manifestation and Truth and the judgments of Israel, without importing different terminology.

Numbers 28:1

  Numbers 28:2-4 — Same-chapter speech introduces scheduled offerings: This speech introduces care for the Lord's offerings at appointed times and specifies the two daily lambs and their morning-evening order.

  Leviticus 23:4 — Appointed festival presentation: Both instructions identify the Lord's feasts as appointed occasions requiring observance at their proper times.

  Exodus 29:38 — Identical daily lamb requirement: Both prescriptions require two unblemished year-old lambs each day as the continual offering to the Lord.

  Exodus 29:40 — Continual lamb accompaniment repeated: The tenth measure of fine flour and quarter hin of oil repeat the earlier prescription for the continual lamb.

Numbers 28:6

  Exodus 29:38-42 — Continual offering institution recalled: The Sinai-linked continual offering recalls the earlier continual lamb instruction, with its daily sequence, accompaniments, and meeting at the Tent.

Numbers 28:7

  Numbers 15:5 — Lamb libation measure with different drink wording: Both rules assign a quarter hin to each lamb, while this verse says strong drink and the earlier instruction says wine.

Numbers 28:8

  Exodus 29:41 — Evening offering matches morning: Both instructions require the second lamb in the evening with the same sacrifice and drink offering as the morning lamb.

  Leviticus 23:3 — Sabbath offering within Sabbath observance: The additional two-lamb offering occurs on the Sabbath, which Leviticus identifies as the seventh-day holy convocation and rest.

Numbers 28:10

Numbers 28:11

  Numbers 28:12-15 — Same-chapter new-moon animals and accompaniments: The new-moon calves, ram, and seven lambs receive the following per-animal flour and wine measures and additional sin goat.

  Numbers 15:6-9 — Calf and ram flour quantities repeated: Both prescriptions assign three tenths of fine flour to the calf and two tenths to the ram, mixed with oil.

Numbers 28:14

  Numbers 15:5-10 — Animal-specific wine measures repeated: Both sets of instructions give a quarter hin of wine for a lamb, a third for a ram, and half for a calf.

Numbers 28:15

  Numbers 28:11 — Same-chapter sin goat added to new-moon animals: The male goat for sin supplements the new-moon whole burnt animals and remains additional to the continual whole burning and its drink offering.

Numbers 28:16

Numbers 28:19

Numbers 28:21

Numbers 28:24

Numbers 28:25

  Leviticus 23:8 — Seventh-day assembly and work restriction: Both laws make the seventh festival day a holy assembly and prohibit laborious work on it.

Numbers 28:26

  Numbers 28:3-8 — Same-chapter weeks additions preserve daily service: The weeks offerings and their drink offerings remain apart from the continual daily offering already prescribed, and must themselves be unblemished.

Numbers 29:1

Numbers 29:2

Numbers 29:3
